Random tangled currents for φ 4 \varphi^{4} : translation invariant Gibbs measures and continuity of the phase transition Institut Camille Jordan (Lyon 1), panis@math.univ-lyon1.fr , severo@math.univ-lyon1.fr
Trishen S. Gunaratnam, Christoforos Panagiotis

Abstract
We prove that the set of automorphism invariant Gibbs measures for the φ 4 \varphi^{4} model on graphs of polynomial growth has at most two extremal measures at all values of β \beta . We also give a sufficient condition to ensure that the set of all Gibbs measures is a singleton. As an application, we show that the spontaneous magnetisation of the nearest-neighbour φ 4 \varphi^{4} model on ℤ d \mathbb{Z}^{d} vanishes at criticality for d ≥ 3 d\geq 3 . The analogous results were established for the Ising model in the seminal works of Aizenman, Duminil-Copin, and Sidoravicius (Comm. Math. Phys., 2015), and Raoufi (Ann. Prob., 2020) using the so-called random current representation introduced by Aizenman (Comm. Math. Phys., 1982). One of the main contributions of this paper is the development of a corresponding geometric representation for the φ 4 \varphi^{4} model called the random tangled current representation.



1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation

The
\varphi^{4}
model is a statistical mechanics model of ferromagnetism with real-valued spins attached to each vertex of a graph whose values are confined according to a quartic single-site potential. Mathematically, this is modelled by a probability measure on real-valued spin configurations on a graph
G=(V,E)
formally given by

\langle F(\varphi)\rangle\,=\frac{1}{Z}\,\int F(\varphi)\,\exp\Big(\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\in E}\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}-\sum_{x\in V}\left(g\varphi_{x}^{4}+a\varphi_{x}^{2}\right)\Big)\textup{d}\varphi

where
g>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
are coupling constants,
\beta>0
is the inverse temperature, and
Z
is a normalisation constant.
This model naturally interpolates between two famous models of statistical physics, namely the Gaussian free field and the Ising model. The Gaussian free field is defined by setting
g=0
and taking appropriate
a
. Formally, in the limit
g\rightarrow 0
when
a>0
, the
\varphi^{4}
model approximates a Gaussian free field of appropriate covariance. On the other hand, the Ising model is defined by integrating instead with the respect to the Bernoulli measure on
\{\pm 1\}
. In this case, the limit
g\rightarrow\infty
for
a<0
formally corresponds to the Ising model. This can be seen easily if one takes a joint scaling limit when
a=-2g
:

\tfrac{1}{2}(\delta_{-1}+\delta_{1})=\lim_{g\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{Z_{g}}e^{-g(\varphi^{2}-1)^{2}}\textup{d}\varphi.

(1.1)

The model has its origins in Euclidean quantum field theory, where its analogue on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is amongst the simplest candidates for a non-trivial (i.e. non-Gaussian) measure. These measures in dimensions
d\geq 2
are a priori ill-defined due to the notorious problem of ultraviolet divergences
[
GJ12
]
. Two major breakthroughs in the constructive field theory programme of the 1960s-1970s were the solutions to these problems in dimensions
2
and
3
due to Nelson
[
Nel66
]
, and Glimm and Jaffe
[
GJ73
]
. In dimensions
d\geq 4
a non-Gaussian measure cannot be obtained due to the triviality results that we mention below.

It was later realised that the
\varphi^{4}
model on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
has importance in statistical physics.
Its viewpoint as a generalised Ising model was first rigorously explored in the landmark paper by Guerra, Rosen, and Simon
[
GRS75
]
.
The approximation to Ising was further exploited by Glimm, Jaffe, and Spencer
[
GJS75
]
, where they adapt the celebrated Peierls’ argument
[
Pei36
]
to prove that the phase transition occurs at a finite non-zero temperature for
\varphi^{4}
in dimensions
d\geq 2
. In particular, there is a critical inverse temperature
\beta_{c}
, depending on the fixed values of
g,a
, such that: for
\beta<\beta_{c}
, the two point correlation tends to
0
; and for
\beta>\beta_{c}
, the two point correlation does not tend to
0
and there is long-range order.

The relationship between
\varphi^{4}
and the Ising model is predicted to be very deep: they are a fundamental example of two models that are believed to belong to the same universality class. Indeed, it is predicted from renormalisation group heuristics (see
[
Gri70
,
Kad93
]
or the recent book
[
BBS19
]
) that at their respective critical points many properties of these two models, such as critical exponents, exactly coincide. A manifestation of this was discovered by Griffiths and Simon in
[
GS73
]
, where they show that the
\varphi^{4}
model arises as a certain near-critical scaling limit of mean-field Ising models. Indeed, this allows one to transfer many special properties of the Ising model, such as correlation inequalities, to
\varphi^{4}
. This makes it one of the simplest non-integrable models to study, even in spite of the unbounded spins.

More direct results towards universality were obtained in dimensions
d\geq 5
by Aizenman
[
Aiz82
]
and Fröhlich
[
Frö82
]
, where triviality of their scaling limits, both in the infinite volume and continuum, were established. The case of
d=4
was settled in the beautiful paper by Aizenman and Duminil-Copin
[
ADC21
]
, where stochastic geometric representations for the model, as in this paper, play a central role. Establishing rigorously universality results in dimensions
2
and
3
remains a difficult challenge. We refer to the recent review
[
Aiz21
]
concerning
\varphi^{4}
for further discussion.

In this article, we are interested in understanding the structure of Gibbs measures for
\varphi^{4}
, and in particular identifying the behaviour at the critical point. To do this, we build on ideas that have led to recent breakthroughs towards these questions in the case of the Ising model. In particular, the continuity of the magnetisation at the critical point in
d\geq 3
was established by Aizenman, Duminil-Copin, and Sidoravicious
[
ADCS15
]
using the so-called random current representation, first introduced by Aizenman in
[
Aiz82
]
. These techniques were further exploited by Raoufi in
[
Rao20
]
to show that the set of translation invariant Gibbs measures on any amenable graph at any temperature is given by convex combinations of two natural extremal measures. This was previously known for temperatures not equal to the critical point on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, see the results of Bodineau
[
Bod06
]
. First, we rigorously define the quantities of interest and state our main result on the structure of Gibbs measures. Then, we state its consequences for continuity of the phase transition (as well as a precise definition of what this means).

1.2
Definitions and structure theorem

Let
G=(V,E)
be a connected, infinite, and locally finite graph. Let
J=(J_{x,y})_{x,y\in V}
be a collection of ferromagnetic interactions, i.e. such that
J_{x,y}\geq 0
for all
x,y\in V
. For
g>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\rho_{g,a}
be the single-site measure on
\mathbb{R}
defined by

\textup{d}\rho_{g,a}(t)=\frac{1}{z_{g,a}}e^{-gt^{4}-at^{2}}\textup{d}t,

(1.2)

where
z_{g,a}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-gt^{4}-at^{2}}\textup{d}t
.
Let
\Lambda\subset V
be finite. Let
h=(h_{x})_{x\in V},\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in V}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
be the external magnetic field and boundary condition, respectively. We define the Hamiltonian

H^{\eta}_{\Lambda,h,J}(\varphi):=-\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda}J_{x,y}\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Lambda\\
y\in V\setminus\Lambda\end{subarray}}J_{x,y}\varphi_{x}\eta_{y}-\sum_{x\in\Lambda}h_{x}\varphi_{x},\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}

(1.3)

whenever

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in V\setminus\Lambda\end{subarray}}J_{x,y}|\eta_{y}|<\infty~~\forall x\in\Lambda
.

The
\varphi^{4}
model on
\Lambda
with coupling constants
(J,g,a)
, boundary condition
\eta
, inverse temperature
\beta>0
, and external magnetic field
h
, is the measure
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h,J,g,a}
defined via the expectation values

\langle f\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h,J,g,a}:=\frac{1}{Z^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h,J,g,a}}\int_{\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}f(\varphi)e^{-\beta H^{\eta}_{\Lambda,hJ}(\varphi)}\textup{d}\rho_{\Lambda,g,a}(\varphi)

(1.4)

where

\textup{d}\rho_{\Lambda,g,a}(\varphi)=\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\textup{d}\rho_{g,a}(\varphi_{x})
,
f:\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a bounded measurable function and
Z^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h,J,g,a}
is the partition function, i.e. the normalisation constant such that
\langle 1\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h,J,g,a}=1
. When clear from context we drop
(J,g,a)
from the notation.

In this article, we impose a restriction on the growth of the graph. We fix
o\in G
a distinguished origin and let
B_{k}
denote the ball of radius
k
centred at
o
with respect to the graph distance
d_{G}
. Let
G
be a
vertex-transitive graph
of
polynomial
growth, i.e. a graph for which there exists
d\geq 1
(called the dimension of
G
) and
c,C>0
such that, for all
k\geq 1
,
we have
ck^{d}\leq|B_{k}|\leq Ck^{d}
.
As such,
G
is amenable:

\inf_{A\subset V,|A|<\infty}\frac{|\partial A|}{|A|}=0

(1.5)

where
\partial A=\{x\in V\setminus A:\exists\,y\in A\text{ with }\{x,y\}\in E\}
and
|\cdot|
denotes cardinality. We fix
\Gamma
a vertex-transitive subgroup of the automorphism group of
G
, denoted Aut
(G)
.

A set of interactions
J=(J_{x,y})_{x,y\in V}
is
admissible
with respect to
G
and
\Gamma
if:

C1

(Ferromagnetic)

J_{x,y}\geq 0
for all
x,y\in V
;

C2

(
\Gamma
-invariance)

J_{\gamma(x),\gamma(y)}=J_{x,y}
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
;

C3

(Irreducibility)
for every
x,y\in V
, there exists a sequence
x_{0}=x,\ldots,x_{k}=y
such that
J_{x_{0},x_{1}}\ldots J_{x_{k-1},x_{k}}>0
;

C4

(Integrability)

\exists\,C>0
and
\epsilon>0
such that
J_{x,y}\leq Cd_{G}(x,y)^{-(d+\epsilon)}
for all
x\neq y\in V
.

We remark that
(C4)
indeed implies that
\sum_{x\in V}J_{o,x}<\infty
, see the calculation in Lemma
D.7
.

We say that a Borel measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{V}
is
tempered
if:

•

for every
\Lambda\subset V
finite,
\nu|_{\Lambda}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
;

•

\nu
is supported on the event

R:=\Big\{(\varphi_{x})_{x\in V}:\exists C\in(0,\infty),\sum_{x\in B_{k}}\varphi_{x}^{2}\leq C|B_{k}|\text{ for all }k\in\mathbb{N}\Big\}.

(1.6)

Definition 1.1

(Gibbs measures)
.

We say that a Borel probability measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{V}
is a Gibbs measure at
(\beta,h)
if it is tempered and for every finite
\Lambda\subset V
and
f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
bounded and measurable, the DLR equation

\nu(f)=\int_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{V}}\langle f\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}\textup{d}\nu(\eta)

(1.7)

holds. In particular, we assume that
\nu
is almost surely supported on
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
such that
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}
is well-defined.

We denote by
\mathcal{G}(\beta,h)
the set of Gibbs measures at
(\beta,h)
. We say that a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{G}(\beta,h)
is
\Gamma
-invariant
if for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the pushforward measure
\gamma_{\ast}\nu
is equal to
\nu
. We denote by
\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta,h)\subset\mathcal{G}(\beta,h)
the set of
\Gamma
-invariant Gibbs measures. The set
\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta,h)
has a structure of a Choquet simplex, i.e. admits a
unique
extremal ergodic decomposition (see
[
Rue70
, Theorem 5.8]
).

Our main result is a structure theorem for
\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta,0)=\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta)
. It states that there are at most two extremal measures
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{+}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{-}
which we define as follows. First, for any
h\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta,h}^{0}=\lim_{\Lambda\uparrow V}\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}^{0}
, which exists by tightness (see Proposition
D.4
) and a standard monotonicity argument using Griffiths’ inequality (see Proposition
3.19
). Define
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{+}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{-}
via the following monotonic weak limits (again by Griffiths’ inequality, see Remark
3.21
):

\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{+}=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta,h}^{0},\qquad\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{-}=\lim_{h\uparrow 0}\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta,h}^{0}.

(1.8)

The measures
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{+}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{-}
are ergodic (in fact mixing) and extremal— see Corollary
2.9
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
G
be a connected, vertex-transitive graph of polynomial growth and
\Gamma\subset\text{Aut}(G)
a vertex-transitive subgroup. Let
J
be an admissible set of coupling constants with respect to
G
and
\Gamma
, and fix
g>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, for any
\beta>0
,

\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta)=\left\{\lambda\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}+(1-\lambda)\langle\cdot\rangle^{-}_{\beta}:\lambda\in[0,1]\right\}.

(1.9)

Moreover, if

\liminf_{d_{G}(o,x)\rightarrow\infty}\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle_{\beta}^{0}=0,

(1.10)

then there exists only one Gibbs measure, i.e.

|\mathcal{G}(\beta)|=|\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta)|=1.

(1.11)

To the best of our knowledge, Theorem
1.2
is the first result that characterises the set of translation invariant Gibbs measures for
\varphi^{4}
at any
\beta
. It has natural consequences for the continuity of the phase transition, i.e. condition (
1.10
) can be verified at criticality in certain cases; these are explored in the next subsection. We believe that certain ideas in the proof may be useful to study the universality between
\varphi^{4}
and Ising, and perhaps of use in studying
P(\varphi)
models (i.e. when the quartic polynomial in the single-site measure is replaced by an arbitrary even polynomial
P
of positive leading coefficient). We also remark that this seems to be the first instance where the
\varphi^{4}
model is studied on general graphs beyond
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and the techniques developed may be useful to study universality with respect to the underlying choice of graph, i.e. microscopic structure. The restriction to (amenable) graphs of polynomial growth is for technical reasons; our proof of the construction of general Gibbs measures works under certain geometric conditions, but we cannot give an example of such an amenable graph which exhibits superpolynomial growth. We refer to Appendix
D
for further discussion. However, the main techniques— in particular all the stochastic geometric tools developed— that we employ should extend to the case of a general amenable graph.

The starting point to prove Theorem
1.2
is a characterisation of the structure of
\Gamma
-invariant Gibbs measures for
\varphi^{4}
— see Proposition
2.15
. This is similar in spirit to the Lebowitz characterisation for the Ising model
[
Leb77
]
, but there are significant complications that arise due to the presence of unbounded spins. The importance of this characterisation is that it reduces the problem to showing that the plus and free measures, i.e.
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{+}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta,h=0}^{0}
, coincide on two-point correlations. Thus, we need to control differences of two-point correlations. For the case of the Ising model
[
ADCS15
,
Rao20
]
this is enabled by the celebrated random current representation, and in particular by the switching lemma
[
Aiz82
, Lemma 3.2]
which allows one to give a probabilistic interpretation of these quantities.

It is well-known that the
\varphi^{4}
model on a
finite
graph
\Lambda
is approximated by an Ising model on
\Lambda\times K_{N}
with special coupling constants, where
K_{N}
is the complete graph on
N
vertices. This is called the Griffiths–Simon approximation
[
GS73
]
. One natural question, in light of the above, is why the naive approach of proving the structure theorem for Gibbs measures directly via the Griffiths–Simon approximation of
\varphi^{4}
fails. This approach would go by proving a corresponding statement for the Ising model on
\Lambda\times K_{N}
and then taking the limit
\Lambda\uparrow G
and
N\to\infty
appropriately. Since the proof in the case of the Ising model heavily relies on ergodicity, it only gives information about the model in infinite volume without any quantitative control in finite volume. Therefore, this approach would require a proof of the convergence of the Griffiths–Simon model on
G\times K_{N}
to the
\varphi^{4}
model on
G
. However, to the best of our knowledge, such a convergence result in the infinite volume is not known. Indeed, the notion of single-site measure, which is crucial to the argument of
[
GS73
]
, disappears in the infinite volume limit.

The core of the paper is the development of a new geometric representation of spin correlations for
\varphi^{4}
, based on the random current representation for the Ising model, that we call a
random tangled current representation
. A current
\mathbf{n}
is a positive integer valued function on the
J
-edges of the graph, meaning the pairs
\{x,y\}\subset V
such that
J_{x,y}>0
. A tangled current is a pair
(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})
obtained as follows: first, the current
\mathbf{n}
is lifted to its natural multigraph
\mathcal{N}
, where each
J
-edge
\{x,y\}
is replaced by
\mathbf{n}_{x,y}
edges. Then, each vertex
x
is replaced by an ordered block of
\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)
vertices, where
\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)
is the degree of
x
in
\mathcal{N}
and each of the
\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)
edges is incident to a distinct vertex in the block. Finally, a choice of an even partition at each vertex,
\mathpzc{t}
, naturally tangles the currents at each block. The key technical tool is the switching principle of Theorem
3.12
, which, in particular, allows us to express ratios and differences of correlation functions in terms of connectivity properties of random tangled currents. With this switching principle, the proof of Theorem
1.2
broadly follows ideas developed in
[
ADCS15
]
and
[
Rao20
]
. However, there are severe obstacles that require a fine knowledge of the behaviour of the single-site tangles, for example that any partition occurs with positive probability. There are also additional essential difficulties caused by the presence of unbounded spin.

The construction of the random tangled currents and the proof of the switching principle starts from the Griffiths–Simon approximation. Indeed, since the model in the prelimit of
N\rightarrow\infty
is an Ising model, we may use the standard switching lemma. Capturing the stable quantities as
N\rightarrow\infty
requires isolating the geometric structures that persist in the scaling limit, which then leads us to random tangled currents. We stress, however, that random tangled currents and the switching principle are stated on the level of
\varphi^{4}
and make no reference to the prelimit; it is only their construction that does. It is an interesting question to prove such a switching principle directly for
\varphi^{4}
, without any reference to the Griffiths–Simon approximation.

Our representation complements the well-known Brydges–Fröhlich–Spencer random walk representation (or BFS representation) of the
\varphi^{4}
model
[
BFS82
]
. This path expansion of the model can be used to derive many classical correlation inequalities for the model (see
[
FFS13
]
and references therein) and also plays a pivotal role in Fröhlich’s proof of triviality
[
Frö82
]
. It would be interesting to see how this object relates to the representation we introduce in this paper. Let us mention that even in the simpler case of the Ising model, the connection between the BFS expansion and the random current representation is not completely obvious. The backbone expansion introduced by Aizenman
[
Aiz82
]
is such a random walk representation, built from explorations of the underlying random current model, but is not quite the same object as the BFS representation. Let us also mention that although it is a very powerful object, it is not clear whether the BFS representation is enough to obtain the results of this paper. The main reason being that we rely heavily on percolation type arguments that cannot be generalised to these random walk models.

1.3
Continuity of the phase transition for
\varphi^{4}
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}

We discuss now an important consequence of Theorem
1.2
to the (continuity of) phase transition for
\varphi^{4}
. We restrict ourselves to the hypercubic lattice
G=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. We fix
g>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
. We also fix a collection of interactions
J
satisfying
(\textbf{C1}
-
\textbf{C4})
.

We begin by defining the phase transition rigorously. There are several natural ways to do this, which are all a posteriori equivalent. We consider here perhaps the most classical definition in terms of spontaneous magnetisation. Other definitions involve existence of long range order (as mentioned above), uniqueness of Gibbs measure, finiteness of susceptibility, etc. We refer the reader to Appendix
C
for these definitions and results relating them.
The spontaneous magnetisation at inverse temperature
\beta
is defined as

m^{*}(\beta):=\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
.
We can now define

\beta_{c}:=\inf\{\beta>0,\text{ }m^{*}(\beta)>0\}.

For
d\geq 2
it has been proved
[
GJS75
]
(see also
[
GJ12
]
) that
\beta_{c}(g,a)\in(0,\infty)
for all
g>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
.

In view of the definition above, it is a natural question to ask whether
m^{*}(\beta_{c})=0
(i.e. the spontaneous magnetisation vanishes at criticality). If so,
m^{*}
is continuous at
\beta_{c}
(see Proposition
A.1
) and the model is said to undergo a
continuous
phase transition. This is a fundamental assumption on which arguments of universality (e.g. via renormalisation group heuristics) are built on.
It turns out that continuity of the phase transition for lattice
\varphi^{4}
models boils down to proving that
|\mathcal{G}(\beta_{c})|=1
, see Proposition
C.2
. Theorem
1.2
gives a sufficient condition (
1.10
) for that to be satisfied.

Very few tools are available to obtain condition (
1.10
) at criticality. For the Ising model in dimension 2, this can be verified by either using exact integrability established in the famous work of Onsager
[
Ons44
]
or planar duality of the interaction as realised by Werner
[
Wer09
]
. However, we stress these techniques only work for the nearest-neighbour model, and have not been extended to finite range interactions in general, or the case of
\varphi^{4}
. In higher dimensions, one can use the lace expansion for either the Ising model
[
Sak07
]
or
\varphi^{4}

[
Sak15
]
. In the intermediate dimensions, such as the physically relevant dimension
3
for nearest-neighbour interactions, the infrared/Gaussian domination bound of Fröhlich, Simon, and Spencer
[
FSS76
]
(see also
[
FILS78
]
) for reflection positive models (see
[
Bis09
, Definition 5.1]
) is the main tool we have at our disposal. As already observed in
[
ADCS15
]
, the infrared bound is a good candidate to ensure continuity.

We now assume that the interaction
J
is admissible and reflection positive— see Section
7
. The canonical examples of reflection positive interactions are

•

(Nearest-neighbor interactions)
J_{x,y}=\delta_{|x-y|_{1}=1}
;

•

(Exponential decay / Yukawa potentials)
J_{x,y}=C\exp(-\mu|x-y|_{1})
for
\mu,C>0
;

•

(Power law decay)
J_{x,y}=C|x-y|_{1}^{-d-\alpha}
for
\alpha,C>0
,

where
|\cdot|_{1}
refers to the
\ell^{1}
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For each
J
, we consider the associated random walk
(X_{k})_{k\geq 0}
with step distribution given by

\mathbb{P}[X_{k+1}=y\>|\>X_{k}=x]=\frac{J_{x,y}}{|J|}

where
|J|:=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}J_{0,x}
. The infrared bound relates the two-point function of a reflection positive model to the Green’s function of the associated random walk
X
. In the case that the random walk is transient, one concludes that the condition (
1.10
) from Theorem
1.2
holds. We then obtain the following result— see Section
7
for the details.

Theorem 1.3

(Continuity of the phase transition)
.

Let
d\geq 1
. Let
J
be admissible, reflection positive, and such that the associated random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is transient. Then,

m^{*}(\beta_{c})=0.

In particular,
m^{*}(\beta_{c})=0
for the nearest-neighbour
\varphi^{4}
model on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
when
d\geq 3
.

Remark 1.4
.

Theorem
1.3
gives that the magnetisation is continuous at
\beta_{c}
when
J
is any admissible and reflection positive interaction in dimension at least
3
. In dimension
2
, it yields the same conclusion when
J
has power law decay of the form
J_{x,y}=C|x-y|_{1}^{-d-\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Finally, in dimension
1
, provided that the model undergoes a non-trivial phase transition
1
1

1

We expect this to be true whenever
J_{x,y}\geq c|x-y|^{-1-\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, as in the case of the one-dimensional Ising model
[
Dys69
]
.
, we get that the magnetisation is continuous at
\beta_{c}
when
J
has power law decay with
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

1.4
Open problems

We now give what, in our opinion, are some beautiful open problems that naturally lead on from Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
. In what follows, we fix
G=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and only consider the nearest-neighbour case.

The most striking omission from Theorem
1.3
is the behaviour at
\beta_{c}
for the nearest-neighbour model in
d=2
. Indeed, the infrared bound is not useful in this context and the analogous result for the Ising model relies on exact integrability
[
Ons44
]
or planar duality
[
Wer09
]
, which only works in the nearest-neighbour case; we stress that the case of the finite range Ising model is open. It is widely believed that the phase transition for
\varphi^{4}
, just as for Ising, is continuous in dimension
2
.

Open Problem 1

(Continuity in
d=2
)
.

Let
d=2
and
J
be the nearest-neighbour interaction. Prove that

m^{*}(\beta_{c})=0.

The only rigorous evidence that we are aware of universality with respect to changes in the Hamiltonian
2
2

2

This is in constrast to universality results with respect to changing the underlying
planar
graph for nearest-neighbour models, i.e. beyond the square lattice, for which much progress has been made. We refer to the review by Chelkak
[
Che18
]
for further details.
for the Ising model in
d=2
are the celebrated works of Pinson and Spencer
[
PS
]
and Giuliani, Greenblatt, and Mastropietro
[
GGM12
]
, which treat the scaling limit of energy-energy correlations for critical finite range Ising models (with a perturbative condition on the range) via renormalisation group techniques; and the work of Aizenman, Duminil-Copin, Tassion, and Warzel
[
ADCTW19
]
, which proves asymptotic Pfaffian relations for boundary point correlations for any critical finite range model (with no perturbative condition) via stochastic geometric techniques. Both cases are coherent with universality, i.e. with the corresponding results established for the critical nearest-neighbour model; see
[
SML64
,
GBK78
]
for the energy-energy and boundary point correlations, respectively. We defer to the articles cited above for precise statements in the case of Ising.

Open Problem 2

(Pfaffian relations in
d=2
)
.

For the nearest-neighbour
\varphi^{4}
model on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, prove that the boundary spin correlations satisfy the Pfaffian relations asymptotically at criticality.

Our final problem concerns the set of
all
Gibbs measures in
d=2
. For the Ising model, it is known
[
Aiz80
,
Hig79
]
that in
d=2
, there are no non-translation invariant Gibbs measures. This is in stark contrast to the existence of (non-translation invariant) Dobrushin measures in
d\geq 3
as established in
[
Dob73
]
. For
\varphi^{4}
, the existence of Dobrushin measures is known in
d\geq 3
, see
[
SV07
]
. The following problem is thus natural.

Open Problem 3

(Aizenman–Higuchi result in
d=2
)
.

Let
d=2
and
J
be the nearest-neighbour interaction. Prove that for all
\beta>\beta_{c}
,

\mathcal{G}(\beta)=\mathcal{G}_{T}(\beta),

where
\mathcal{G}_{T}(\beta)
is the set of translation invariant Gibbs measures.

2
Gibbs measures and correlation functions

In this section, we prove important properties about Gibbs measures. First, we construct the plus and minus measures, and show that they are mixing and extremal. Then, we provide a Lebowitz-type characterisation on the structure of
\Gamma
-invariant Gibbs measures that reduces the proof of Theorem
1.2
to checking that even correlations under the plus and free measures coincide. We fix
\beta>0
.

2.1
Regularity of Gibbs measures

It is crucial to our techniques that
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle^{-}_{\beta}
are realised as infinite volume limits of measures with appropriate boundary conditions at
h=0
. This is not a priori clear from their definitions via limits as
h\downarrow 0
and
h\uparrow 0
of
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta,h}^{0}
, respectively. Indeed, it is not even clear that they are even Gibbs measures at
h=0
. As such, we show that any Gibbs measure satisfies a regularity condition that puts a constraint on the growth of the configurations that it is supported on. This allows us to define natural analogues of plus and minus boundary condition measures obtained from finite volume measures that are mixing and extremal, which we shall do in the next subsection.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\nu
be a measure on
\mathbb{R}^{V}
. For
\gamma,\delta>0
, we say that
\nu
is
(\gamma,\delta)
-regular if, for every
\Lambda\subset V
finite, the density of the projection measure satisfies

\frac{\textup{d}\nu}{\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda}}(\varphi_{\Lambda})\leq e^{-\sum_{x\in\Lambda}(\gamma\varphi_{x}^{4}-\delta)},

(2.1)

where
\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
.

The following proposition states that (tempered) Gibbs measures are regular. The proof is technical and relies on estimates developed in
[
LP76
]
, adapted to our more general setting on graphs of polynomial growth. We therefore defer the proof to Appendix
D
, c.f. Proposition
D.6
.

Proposition 2.2
.

There exist constants
\gamma,\delta>0
depending on
G,g,a,J,\beta,h
such that
\nu
is
(\gamma,\delta)
-regular for every
\nu\in\mathcal{G}(\beta)
. Moreover, the constants
\gamma
and
\delta
are continuous in
g,a,J,\beta,h
.

Regular measures concentrate on weakly growing configurations.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\nu
be
(\gamma,\delta)
-regular. For every
M=M(\gamma,\delta)>0
sufficiently large, we have

\nu[\Xi(M)]=1,

where

\Xi(M):=\{\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{V}:\exists\Lambda_{\varphi}\subset V\text{ finite},~\varphi^{4}_{x}\leq M\log d_{G}(o,x)~~\forall x\in V\setminus\Lambda_{\varphi}\}.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Definition
2.1
, a union bound, and Borel-Cantelli.
∎

In light of Lemma
2.3
, there is a natural notion of “maximal boundary condition”.

Definition 2.4
.

For
M>0
, we define the configurations
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}(M)
and
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}(M)
as

\mathfrak{p}_{x}=\sqrt[4]{M\log\big(d_{G}(o,x)\vee 1\big)},\qquad\forall x\in V,

and
\mathfrak{m}=-\mathfrak{p}
. We refer to
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\mathfrak{m}}_{\Lambda,\beta}
as the plus and minus (finite volume) measures.

Remark 2.5
.

Proposition
2.2
and Lemma
2.3
imply that any Gibbs measure is a limit of finite volume (regular)
\varphi^{4}
measures
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\eta}
with (possibly random) boundary conditions
\eta
satisfying
|\eta_{x}|\leq\mathfrak{p}_{x}(M)~~\forall x\in V\setminus\Lambda
, provided
M
is taken sufficiently large. This is established in Appendix
D
. One of the key results to proving Proposition
2.2
is that finite volume measures with boundary condition satisfying
|\eta|\leq\mathfrak{p}
satisfy an analogous estimate to (
2.1
) with
(\gamma,\delta)
uniform over subsets sufficiently far away from the boundary, see Corollary
D.12
for a precise statement. Conversely, any sequence of finite volume measures with boundary conditions satisfying
|\eta|\leq\mathfrak{p}
is tight and any accumulation point is a Gibbs measure, see Propositions
D.4
and
D.5
.

2.2
Extremality and mixing of the plus and minus measures

Recall that
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle^{-}_{\beta}
were defined as the respective monotone limits
\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\langle\cdot\rangle^{0}_{\beta,h}
and
\lim_{h\uparrow 0}\langle\cdot\rangle^{0}_{\beta,h}
. We now show that they can be obtained by taking infinite volume limits of measures with maximal boundary conditions at
(\beta,h=0)
, thus giving a proof that they are in
\mathcal{G}(\beta)
.

Proposition 2.6
.

For
M
sufficiently large, one has that
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\mathfrak{p}}\to\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{+}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\mathfrak{m}}\to\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{-}
as
\Lambda\uparrow V
. Moreover, the measures are maximal and minimal, respectively, in the sense that, for any
\langle\cdot\rangle\in\mathcal{G}(\beta)
and any increasing and integrable function
f
,

\langle f\rangle^{-}_{\beta}\leq\langle f\rangle\leq\langle f\rangle^{+}_{\beta}.

(2.2)

Proof.

We only prove the convergence results; the monotonicity (
2.2
) is a direct consequence of the convergence together with Remark
2.5
, and the monotonicity on boundary conditions in finite volume (see Proposition
B.4
). Without loss of generality, we only consider the case of
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
.
First, since the measures
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\mathfrak{p}}
are uniformly regular (see Remark
2.5
), it is straightforward to prove that they form a tight sequence of measures. Now, let
\Lambda_{n}\uparrow V
be any sequence such that
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}
converges to a probability measure, which we denote by
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\beta}
. Note that this is a Gibbs measure. We prove that
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\beta}=\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
, which in particular implies that the full sequence
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\mathfrak{p}}
converges.

By monotonicity
3
3

3

This follows by differentiating in
h
and using the FKG inequality of Proposition
B.1
with the observation that the identity map
\varphi_{x}\mapsto\varphi_{x}
is increasing.
in
h
, for any
f
local, increasing and bounded, and
h>0
,

\langle f\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\beta}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\langle f\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\langle f\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta,h}=\nu_{\beta,h}[f],

where
\nu_{\beta,h}:=\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta,h}
is the unique measure in
\mathcal{G}(\beta,h)
for
h\neq 0
: this is a consequence of the Lee–Yang theorem for the
\varphi^{4}
model
[
GS73
]
and
[
LP76
, Theorem 5.6]
. Since by definition
\nu_{\beta,h}\to\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{+}
as
h\to 0
, we conclude that

\langle f\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\beta}\leq\langle f\rangle_{\beta}^{+}.

For the reverse inequality, let
f
be local, increasing, and bounded. For
h>0
and
\Lambda\subset V
finite, let

\varepsilon(\Lambda,\beta,h):=\|f\|_{\infty}\nu_{\beta,h}\big[\exists x\notin\Lambda:\>|\eta_{x}|>\mathfrak{p}_{x}(M)\big].

Provided
M
is large enough, by continuity of the regularity coefficients and concentration on regular boundary conditions,
\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\varepsilon(\Lambda,h)=o(1)
as
\Lambda\uparrow V
. Thus, by the DLR equations and monotonicity in boundary conditions (see Proposition
B.4
), we have for
n
sufficiently large

\displaystyle\langle f\rangle^{+}_{\beta}

\displaystyle:=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}\nu_{\beta,h}[f]=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}\int\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda_{n}}^{\eta}\,\textup{d}\nu_{\beta,h}(\eta)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left(\varepsilon(\Lambda_{n},\beta,h)+\int\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta,h}^{\mathfrak{p}}\textup{d}\nu_{\beta,h}(\eta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\varepsilon(\Lambda_{n},\beta,h)+\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta,h}^{\mathfrak{p}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\langle f\rangle_{\beta}^{\mathfrak{p}}.

Therefore,
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\beta}
agree on all local bounded increasing functions, hence
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\beta}=\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
, as we wanted to prove.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

Unlike the Ising model, we do not have clear monotonicity in the volume of the quantity
\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\mathfrak{p}}
(for
f
local, increasing and bounded). This is a consequence of the unboundedness of the spins. As a result, we must use regularity to “create” a maximal boundary condition
\mathfrak{p}
, which comes with an error term, and temperedness to bound the spins inside a large box. As a byproduct of the methods developed above, we see that for
\Lambda_{1}\subset\Lambda_{2}
two finite subsets of
V
, and for
f
local (with support in
\Lambda_{1}
), increasing, bounded,

\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda_{2},\beta}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda_{1},\beta}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\varepsilon(\Lambda_{1}),

where
\varepsilon(\Lambda_{1})\rightarrow 0
as
\Lambda_{1}\uparrow V
.

In what follows, we fix
M
sufficiently large so that Lemma
2.3
and Proposition
2.6
apply.

Corollary 2.8

(Extremality)
.

The measures
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle^{-}_{\beta}
are extremal in the following sense. Let
\#\in\{+,-\}
. If there exists
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{G}(\beta)
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
such that

\langle\cdot\rangle^{\#}_{\beta}=\lambda\nu_{1}+(1-\lambda)\nu_{2},

then necessarily
\nu_{1}=\nu_{2}=\langle\cdot\rangle^{\#}_{\beta}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of (
2.2
).
∎

As a standard consequence of the extremality of the Gibbs measures proved in Corollary
2.8
, we obtain that the measures
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle^{-}_{\beta}
are mixing. See for example
[
Geo11
, Theorem 7.7 and Proposition 7.9]
.

Corollary 2.9

(Mixing)
.

Let
\#\in\{+,-\}
. Then, for all local bounded functions
f
and
g
,

\lim_{d_{G}(o,x)\rightarrow\infty}\langle f\cdot\gamma_{x}g\rangle^{\#}_{\beta}=\langle f\rangle^{\#}_{\beta}\langle g\rangle^{\#}_{\beta}

for all
\gamma_{x}\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma_{x}o=x
.

2.3
Ergodicity of the free measure under even functions

Recall that the free measure, denoted
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{0}
, is the weak limit of
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{0}
as
\Lambda\uparrow V
. The free measure is a Gibbs measure. Indeed, by Remark
2.5
, we have tightness and that the (unique) limit point is tempered and satisfies the DLR equations.

Proposition 2.10
.

The measure
\langle\cdot\rangle^{0}_{\beta}
is mixing on even functions in the following sense. For all local even functions
f
and
g
such that
f,g
are square integrable,

\lim_{d_{G}(o,x)\rightarrow\infty}\langle f\cdot\gamma_{x}g\rangle^{0}_{\beta}=\langle f\rangle^{0}_{\beta}\langle g\rangle^{0}_{\beta}

for all
\gamma_{x}\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma_{x}o=x
.

Proof.

Let
A:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be of finite support and such that
\sum_{x}A_{x}
is even. Define
\varphi_{A}:=\prod_{x\in V}\varphi_{x}^{A_{x}}
. Let
(\tilde{J}_{x,y})_{x,y\in V}
be a set of interactions supported on finitely many pairs in
V
such that
-J_{x,y}\leq\tilde{J}_{x,y}\leq 0
.
We define

H_{\tilde{J}}(\varphi)=-\sum_{\{x,y\}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(V)}\tilde{J}_{x,y}\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y},

where
\mathcal{P}_{2}(V)=\big\{\{x,y\}:x,y\in V\big\}
. Note that, by taking infinite volume limits, the measure
\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\beta,J+\gamma_{x}^{-1}\tilde{J}}^{0}
is well-defined and satisfies

\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\beta,J+\gamma_{x}^{-1}\tilde{J}}^{0}=\frac{\langle\varphi_{A}e^{-\beta H_{\tilde{J}}(\gamma_{x}\varphi)}\rangle_{\beta,J}^{0}}{\langle e^{-\beta H_{\tilde{J}}(\gamma_{x}\varphi)}\rangle_{\beta,J}^{0}}.

Now for
x\in V
such that
n:=d_{G}(o,x)
is sufficiently large, by monotonicity in the volume and Griffiths’ inequality, we have that

\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle^{0}_{B_{n/2},\beta,J}=\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{B_{n/2},\beta,J+\gamma_{x}^{-1}\tilde{J}}^{0}\leq\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\beta,J+\gamma_{x}^{-1}\tilde{J}}^{0}\leq\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\beta,J}^{0}.

Hence, by taking
d_{G}(o,x)\rightarrow\infty
and using convergence of correlations in the infinite volume limit, we have shown that

\lim_{d_{G}(o,x)\rightarrow\infty}\langle\varphi_{A}e^{-\beta H_{\tilde{J}}(\gamma_{x}\varphi)}\rangle_{\beta,J}^{0}=\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\beta,J}^{0}\langle e^{-\beta H_{\tilde{J}}(\varphi)}\rangle_{\beta,J}^{0}.

This establishes Proposition
2.10
for
f(\varphi)=\varphi_{A}
and
g(\varphi)=e^{-\beta H_{\tilde{J}}(\varphi)}
. By standard approximation arguments via Taylor expanding the exponential, resumming, and using the Stone–Weierstrass theorem, the mixing extends to all even functions.
∎

2.4
A characterisation of
\Gamma
-invariant Gibbs measures via correlations

The main result of this subsection is a characterisation for the structure of the
\Gamma
-invariant Gibbs measures, see Proposition
2.15
. The analogous result for the Ising model was obtained by Lebowitz
[
Leb77
]
. In our setting, there are significant complications that come from the unboundedness of the spins.

A central tool in the proof is the following correlation inequality, which was first established in
[
Leb77
]
. Recall that if
A:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is finitely supported, we denote

\varphi_{A}=\prod_{x\in V}\varphi_{x}^{A_{x}}.

Proposition 2.11

(Ginibre inequality)
.

Let
A,B:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be finitely supported. Then, if
\Lambda
contains the support of
A
and
B
, for any
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\Xi(M)
such that
|\eta_{x}|\leq\eta^{\prime}_{x}
for all
x\notin\Lambda
,

\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\eta^{\prime}}_{\Lambda,\beta}-\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}\geq\left|\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle^{\eta^{\prime}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}-\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\eta}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\eta^{\prime}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\right|.

Moreover, if
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}\in\mathcal{G}(\beta)
,

\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}-\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\beta}\geq\left|\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\beta}-\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\beta}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}\right|.

Proof.

We include a proof in Appendix
B.2
for completeness.
∎

We make heavy use of the fact that the sign field of the
\varphi^{4}
measure can be viewed as an Ising model with random coupling constants coming from the absolute value field. Given
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the sign of
x
as
\textup{sgn}(x)=x/|x|
for
x\neq 0
, and
\textup{sgn}(0)=1
. We have the following coupling result.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\Lambda\subset V
be a finite set and let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
such that
|\eta|\leq\mathfrak{p}
(outside
\Lambda
). There exists a coupling
(\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta},\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}},\varphi^{\eta})
,
\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}
,
\varphi^{\eta}\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}
and a non-decreasing function
g:(0,\infty)^{2}\mapsto(0,\infty)
, such that
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}
-almost surely,

|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|\geq|\varphi^{\eta}_{x}|\quad\text{for every $x\in\Lambda$},

\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x})\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y})\mid|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}|,|\varphi^{\eta}|\right]\geq g(|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|,|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y}|)\quad\text{for every $x,y\in\Lambda$},

and

\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x})\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y})-\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\eta}_{x})\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\eta}_{y})\mid|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}|,|\varphi^{\eta}|\right]\geq 0\quad\text{for every $x,y\in\Lambda$}.

The proof of this result requires the following lemma.

Lemma 2.13

(Stochastic domination of absolute value fields)
.

Let
\Lambda\subset V
be a finite set, let
\beta>0
, and let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
such that
|\eta|\leq\mathfrak{p}
(outside
\Lambda
). Then
\langle|\cdot|\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}
stochastically dominates
\langle|\cdot|\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}
, in the sense that for any local increasing and bounded function
f
,

\langle f(|\varphi|)\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\mathfrak{p}}\geq\langle f(|\varphi|)\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\eta}.

Proof.

Let

J^{\eta}(|\varphi|)_{x,y}=\begin{cases}J_{x,y}|\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}|,&\text{for }x,y\in\Lambda\\
J_{x,y}|\varphi_{x}\eta_{y}|,&\text{for }x\in\Lambda,y\in V\setminus\Lambda.\end{cases}

It follows from a direct calculation that

\textup{d}\langle\cdot\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}(|\varphi|)=\frac{Z^{\textup{Ising},\textup{sgn}(\eta)}_{\Lambda,J^{\eta}(|\varphi|),\beta}}{Z^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\textup{d}\rho(|\varphi_{x}|),

where, for any
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}
,

Z^{\textup{Ising},\tau}_{\Lambda,J^{\eta}(|\varphi|),\beta}:=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}}e^{-\beta H^{\textup{Ising},\tau}_{\Lambda,J^{\eta}(|\varphi|)}(\sigma)}

and for
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}

H^{\textup{Ising},\tau}_{\Lambda,J^{\eta}(|\varphi|)}(\sigma):=-\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda}J^{\eta}(|\varphi|)_{x,y}\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Lambda\\
y\in V\setminus\Lambda\end{subarray}}J^{\eta}(|\varphi|)_{x,y}\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}.

For simplicity, we write
+
for the configuration
\tau_{x}=1
,
\forall x\in V
.

Thus, for any decreasing function
F:[0,\infty)^{\Lambda}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,

\langle F(|\varphi|)\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}=\frac{\big\langle F(|\varphi|)G(|\varphi|)\big\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}}{\big\langle G(|\varphi|)\big\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}},

where
G(|\varphi|):=\frac{Z^{\textup{Ising},\textup{sgn}(\eta)}_{\Lambda,J^{\eta}(|\varphi|),\beta}}{Z^{\textup{Ising},+}_{\Lambda,J^{\mathfrak{p}}(|\varphi|),\beta}}.

Note that
G(|\varphi|)
is a decreasing function of
|\varphi|
, which follows from taking the logarithmic derivative and using the Ginibre inequality for the Ising model (see
[
Leb77
]
). We can now use the FKG inequality for the absolute value field (see Proposition
B.2
) to conclude that

\langle F(|\varphi|)\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}\geq\langle F(|\varphi|)\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta},

as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.12
.

Using Lemma
2.13
and Strassen’s theorem
[
Str65
]
, we obtain a coupling
(\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta},\psi^{\mathfrak{p}},\psi^{\eta})
, where
\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}\sim\langle|\cdot|\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda}
and
\psi^{\eta}\sim\langle|\cdot|\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda}
, such that
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}
-almost surely,
\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}\geq\psi^{\eta}_{x}
for every
x\in\Lambda
. Recall the notation introduced in the proof of Lemma
2.13
.
Enlarging our probability space, we can assume that in the same probability space, there is a family
I=\{\sigma^{\mathfrak{p}}(\textbf{J})\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{\textup{Ising},+}_{\Lambda,\textbf{J},\beta},\sigma^{\eta}(\textbf{J})\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{\textup{Ising},\textup{sgn}(\eta)}_{\Lambda,\textbf{J},\beta}\mid\textbf{J}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\Lambda)\mapsto[0,\infty)\}
of independent Ising models that are also independent from
\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\psi^{\eta}
. Now let
\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}:=\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\sigma^{\mathfrak{p}}(J^{\mathfrak{p}}(\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}))
and
\varphi^{\eta}:=\psi^{\eta}\cdot\sigma^{\eta}(J^{\eta}(\psi^{\eta}))
. It follows that
\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda}
,
\varphi^{\eta}\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda}
, and

|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|\geq|\varphi^{\eta}_{x}|
for every
x\in\Lambda
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}

\displaystyle\left[\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x})\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y})-\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\eta}_{x})\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\eta}_{y})\mid|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}|,|\varphi^{\eta}|\right]

\displaystyle=\langle\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}\rangle^{\textup{Ising},+}_{\Lambda,J^{\mathfrak{p}}(\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}),\beta}-\langle\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}\rangle^{\textup{Ising},\textup{sgn}(\eta)}_{\Lambda,J^{\eta}(\psi^{\eta}),\beta}\geq 0,

where the equality follows from independence and the inequality from the Ginibre
inequality for the Ising model. Similarly, by the monotonicity properties of the Ising model,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x})\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y})\mid|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}|,|\varphi^{\eta}|\right]

\displaystyle=\langle\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}\rangle^{\textup{Ising},+}_{\Lambda,J^{\mathfrak{p}}(\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}),\beta}

\displaystyle\geq g(\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x},\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y}):=\langle\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}\rangle^{\textup{Ising},0}_{\{x,y\},J^{0}(\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}),\beta}.

The latter is strictly positive by the random current expansion of the Ising model, and non-decreasing by Griffiths’ inequality.
∎

The following Lemma is the crucial place in this section where
\Gamma
-invariance is required.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\beta>0
. Let
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta)
. Then, for any
x,y\in V
such that
J_{x,y}>0
,

\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta}^{+}\geq\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta}\geq\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta}^{0}.

(2.3)

Proof.

The first inequality in (
2.3
) is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.11
. The second inequality is more involved and requires
\Gamma
-invariance. We show that a change in temperature is more costly than a change in boundary condition. More precisely, define the function
t\mapsto(\tilde{J}_{u,v}(t))_{u,v\in V}
as
\tilde{J}_{u,v}(t)=tJ_{u,v}
if there is
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
u=\gamma x
and
v=\gamma y
, and
\tilde{J}_{u,v}(t)=J_{u,v}
otherwise. For
t\in(0,1)
, define
\alpha(t):=\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{+}_{\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}
. In addition, define
b=\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta}
. Assume that, for any
t\in(0,1)
,

\alpha(t)\leq b.

(2.4)

We now argue that the second inequality in (
2.3
) is satisfied. Indeed, as a consequence of the Ginibre inequality of Proposition
2.11
, we have that
\alpha(t)\geq\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}^{0}
. The desired inequality then follows from the left-continuity
4
4

4

This follows from the fact that this function can be realised as a increasing limit of increasing functions.
at
t=1
of
t\mapsto\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}^{0}
. It remains to prove (
2.4
).

Fix
t\in(0,1)
and let
\alpha=\alpha(t)
. Let
S_{n}
be the set of pairs
u,v\in B_{n}
such that there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
\gamma x=u
and
\gamma y=v
. Note that there exists
c_{1}>0
such that
|S_{n}|\geq c_{1}|B_{n}|
. We define

f(n):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{u,v\}\in S_{n}\end{subarray}}\varphi_{u}\varphi_{v}.

Note that by
\Gamma
-invariance, we have

\langle f(n)\rangle_{\beta}=b|S_{n}|,\text{ and }\langle f(n)\rangle_{\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}=\alpha|S_{n}|.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\alpha)
. Define the event

C_{n}=\{f(n)\leq(\alpha-2\varepsilon_{0})|S_{n}|\}.

The key result we need to establish is the following large deviation estimate: there exists
c>0
such that, for
n
sufficiently large

\big\langle\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\mathbbm{1}_{K_{n}}\big\rangle_{\beta}\leq e^{-c|B_{n}|},

(2.5)

where
K_{n}=\{|\eta|\leq\mathfrak{p}\text{ in }B_{n}^{c}\}
. We assume this for now to establish (
2.4
).

On the one hand, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and (
2.5
), we have that

\big\langle\langle f(n)\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\mathbbm{1}_{K_{n}}\big\rangle_{\beta}=o(1).

On the other hand, by a union bound and regularity,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbbm{1}_{\{\exists x\notin B_{n},\>|\eta_{x}|>\mathfrak{p}_{x}\}}\rangle_{\beta}^{+}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\notin B_{n}}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\eta_{x}|\geq\mathfrak{p}_{x}\}}\rangle_{\beta}^{+}\leq\sum_{x\notin B_{n}}\frac{1}{d_{G}(o,x)^{M}}\lesssim\frac{1}{n^{M-d}}.

Thus, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, for every
M
large enough,

\left|\left\langle\langle f(n)\rangle_{B_{n},\beta,J}^{\eta}\mathbbm{1}_{K_{n}^{c}}\right\rangle_{\beta}\right|\leq\sqrt{\langle f(n)^{2}\rangle_{\beta}}\sqrt{\langle\mathbbm{1}_{\exists x\notin B_{n},\>|\eta_{x}|>\mathfrak{p}_{x}}\rangle_{\beta}}=o(|S_{n}|).

Hence, by the above estimates and the definition of
C_{n}
,

\displaystyle b|S_{n}|

\displaystyle=\langle f(n)\rangle_{\beta}=\langle\langle f(n)\rangle_{B_{n},\beta,J}^{\eta}\mathbbm{1}_{K_{n}}\rangle_{\beta}+\langle\langle f(n)\rangle_{B_{n},\beta,J}^{\eta}\mathbbm{1}_{K_{n}^{c}}\rangle_{\beta}

\displaystyle\geq(\alpha-2\varepsilon_{0})|S_{n}|\big\langle\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}^{c}}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\mathbbm{1}_{K_{n}}\big\rangle_{\beta}-o(|S_{n}|)

\displaystyle=(\alpha-2\varepsilon_{0})|S_{n}|\left(\langle\mathbbm{1}_{K_{n}}\rangle_{\beta}-\big\langle\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\mathbbm{1}_{K_{n}}\big\rangle_{\beta}\right)-o(|S_{n}|).

As a result, by applying (
2.5
),

b|S_{n}|\geq(\alpha-2\varepsilon_{0})|S_{n}|-o(|S_{n}|).

Sending
n
to infinity we obtain
b\geq\alpha-2\varepsilon_{0}
, which implies that
b\geq\alpha
, as desired.

Finally, we turn to the proof of the large deviation estimate (
2.5
). By the DLR equations we have

\langle f(n)\rangle_{B_{n},\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\geq\langle f(n)\rangle^{+}_{\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}-\varepsilon(n)=\alpha|S_{n}|-\varepsilon(n)

where, arguing as above,

\varepsilon(n):=\langle|f(n)|\mathbbm{1}_{\{\exists x\notin B_{n},\>|\eta_{x}|>\mathfrak{p}_{x}\}}\rangle^{+}_{\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}=o(|S_{n}|).

Define

A_{n}=\{f(n)\geq(\alpha-\varepsilon_{0})|S_{n}|\}.

For every
n
large enough, the above considerations imply

\langle f(n)\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}=\langle f(n)\rangle_{B_{n},\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}^{\mathfrak{p}}-\langle f(n)\mathbbm{1}_{A^{c}_{n}}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}\geq\varepsilon_{0}|S_{n}|-o(|S_{n}|)\geq\varepsilon_{0}|S_{n}|/2.

Observe, furthermore, that

\displaystyle\langle f(n)\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,\tilde{J}(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}e^{\beta(t-1)f(n)}f(n)\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta,J}}{\langle e^{\beta(t-1)f(n)}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,J}}\leq\frac{\langle\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}e^{\beta(t-1)f(n)}|f(n)|\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta,J}}{\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}e^{\beta(t-1)f(n)}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,J}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\beta\varepsilon_{0}(t-1)|S_{n}|}\frac{\langle|f(n)|\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,J}}{\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,J}}.

Since
\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\rightarrow\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta,J}^{+}
, we have

\langle|f(n)|\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\leq|S_{n}|\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{B_{n},\beta,J}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq C_{1}|S_{n}|
for some constant
C_{1}>0
independent of
n
. Re-arranging, this yields a bound on
\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,J}
. We want to replace the boundary
\mathfrak{p}
by an arbitrary boundary condition in
K_{n}
.

Let
\eta\in K_{n}
. Define

g_{n}(\eta):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in B_{n}\\
y\notin B_{n}\end{subarray}}J_{x,y}\varphi_{x}(\mathfrak{p}_{y}-\eta_{y}).

Using the above inequalities, we have that

\frac{\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}e^{\beta g_{n}(\eta)}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}}{\langle e^{\beta g_{n}(\eta)}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}}=\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\leq\frac{2C_{1}}{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{\beta\varepsilon_{0}}{c_{1}}(1-t)|B_{n}|}.

(2.6)

Note that by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality

\Big(\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\Big)^{2}\leq\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}e^{\beta g_{n}(\eta)}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\langle e^{-\beta g_{n}(\eta)}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J},

so that,

\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2C_{1}}{\varepsilon_{0}}}e^{-\frac{\beta\varepsilon_{0}}{2c_{1}}(1-t)|B_{n}|}\langle e^{\beta|g_{n}(\eta)|}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}.

(2.7)

We seek an estimate on
\langle e^{\beta|g_{n}(\eta)|}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}
which is uniform over
\eta\in K
. To this end, observe that, by the definition of
\mathfrak{p}
, and by the condition
(\textbf{C4})
satisfied by
J
,

\displaystyle|g_{n}(\eta)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in B_{n}\\
y\notin B_{n}\end{subarray}}J_{x,y}|\varphi_{x}|\mathfrak{p}_{y}

\displaystyle\leq 2C\sum_{x\in B_{n}}|\varphi_{x}|\sum_{y\notin B_{n}}\frac{\sqrt[4]{M\log d_{G}(o,y)}}{d_{G}(x,y)^{d+\varepsilon}}.

Let
\delta>0
be such that
|B_{n}\setminus B_{n-1}|=O(n^{-\delta}|B_{n}|)
for every
n\geq 1
(such a
\delta
exists by
[
Tes07
]
— see Remark
D.2
in the Appendix).
Note that for every
x\in B_{n}
and
y\not\in B_{n}
, using that
d_{G}(x,y)\geq 1
and
d_{G}(o,x)\leq n
, we have
d_{G}(o,y)\leq d_{G}(o,x)+d_{G}(x,y)\leq(n+1)\,d_{G}(x,y)
.

Moreover, for every
x\in B_{n}
and
y\not\in B_{n}
, if
d_{G}(o,x)\leq n-n^{\delta/2}
, then since
d_{G}(o,y)\geq n
we have
d_{G}(x,y)\geq n^{\delta/2}
. Therefore, with the same condition on
d_{G}(o,x)
, we have

\frac{\sqrt[4]{M\log d_{G}(o,y)}}{d_{G}(x,y)^{d+\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{\sqrt[4]{M\log(n+1)+M\log d_{G}(x,y)}}{d_{G}(x,y)^{d+\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{1}{d_{G}(x,y)^{d+\varepsilon/2}}

provided that
n
is large enough. By Lemma
D.8
, the sum

\sum_{y\in V\setminus\{x\}}d_{G}(x,y)^{-d-\varepsilon/2}
is finite.
Thus, combining the above estimates and summing over
y
, we find

|g_{n}(\eta)|\leq C^{\prime}\sum_{x\in B_{n}}|\varphi_{x}|\left(\sqrt[4]{M\log n}\mathbbm{1}_{x\in B_{n}\setminus B_{c_{n}}}+o(1)\right)

(2.8)

for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
, where
c_{n}=\lfloor n-n^{\delta/2}\rfloor
and
o(1)
does not depend
\eta\in K_{n}
. Plugging this inequality into (
2.7
) and integrating over
K_{n}
yields

\big\langle\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{n}}\rangle^{\eta}_{B_{n},\beta,J}\mathbbm{1}_{K_{n}}\big\rangle_{\beta}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2C_{1}}{\varepsilon_{0}}}e^{-\frac{\beta\varepsilon_{0}}{2c_{1}}(1-t)|B_{n}|}\left\langle e^{\beta C^{\prime}\sqrt[4]{M\log n}\sum_{x\in B_{n}\setminus B_{c_{n}}}|\varphi_{x}|+o(1)\sum_{x\in B_{n}}|\varphi_{x}|}\right\rangle_{\beta}.

By regularity of
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}
,

\left\langle e^{\beta C^{\prime}\sqrt[4]{M\log n}\sum_{x\in B_{n}\setminus B_{c_{n}}}|\varphi_{x}|+o(1)\sum_{x\in B_{n}}|\varphi_{x}|}\right\rangle_{\beta}=e^{o(|B_{n}|)},

thus establishing (
2.5
) to complete the proof of (
2.3
).
∎

We now turn to the main result of this section.

Proposition 2.15

(Lebowitz-type characterisation)
.

Let
\beta>0
.
The following are equivalent.

1.

For any
\Gamma
-invariant Gibbs measure
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta)
, there exists
\lambda\in[0,1]
such that

\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}=\lambda\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}+(1-\lambda)\langle\cdot\rangle^{-}_{\beta}
.

2.

For any
\Gamma
-invariant Gibbs measure
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta)
, and for any bounded and measurable even function
f
, we have

\langle f\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=\langle f\rangle_{\beta}
.

3.

For any
\Gamma
-invariant Gibbs measure
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta)
, and for any
x,y\in V
such that
J_{x,y}>0
, any vertices
z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{k}\in V
,
k\geq 0
, and any positive integers
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k}
, we have

\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}.

4.

For any
x,y\in V
such that
J_{x,y}>0
,

\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{0}_{\beta}.

Proof.

Since
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{+}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{-}
coincide on even functions, it is straightforward that
1\Rightarrow 4
.

Let us prove that
4\Rightarrow 3
. To this end, by Lemma
2.14
we have that for any
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta)
,

\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta}.

Consider some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and a finite set
\Lambda
that contains
x,y,z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
. Let

\displaystyle A_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle:=\{\eta:|\eta_{z}|\leq\mathfrak{p}_{z}\>\forall z\in\Lambda^{c},\>\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\leq\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\text{ and }\langle|\varphi_{w}|\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\leq\langle|\varphi_{w}|\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}+\varepsilon,\>\forall\,w=x,y,z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\}.

We first claim that
\langle\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\Lambda}}\rangle_{\beta}\rightarrow 1
as
\Lambda\uparrow V
. Indeed, it suffices to show that
\langle|\varphi_{x}|\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\leq\langle|\varphi_{x}|\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}+\varepsilon

with probability tending to
1
as
\Lambda\uparrow V
. To this end, let

S_{\Lambda}=\{\eta:|\eta_{z}|\leq\mathfrak{p}_{z}\>\forall z\in\Lambda^{c},\;\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\leq\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}+\delta_{1}\},

where
\delta_{1}>0
is a constant to be determined, and note that
\langle\mathbbm{1}_{S_{\Lambda}}\rangle\rightarrow 1
as
\Lambda\uparrow V
. Fix some
\eta\in S_{\Lambda}
. Now recall the coupling
(\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta},\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}},\varphi^{\eta})
of Lemma
2.12
. Then,

\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}-\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}\geq\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|-|\varphi^{\eta}_{x}|\right)\,|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y}|\,g(|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|,|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y}|)\right].

Letting
E=\{|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|-|\varphi^{\eta}_{x}|\geq\delta_{2},\,|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|\geq\delta_{2},\,|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y}|\geq\delta_{2}\}
, where
\delta_{2}>0
is a constant to be determined, we obtain that

\delta_{1}\geq\delta_{2}^{2}\,g(\delta_{2},\delta_{2})\,\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}[E].

By decomposing and applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality we obtain

\langle|\varphi_{x}|\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\leq\sqrt{\langle\varphi^{2}_{x}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}}\sqrt{\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[E\cup\{|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|\leq\delta_{2}\}\cup\{|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y}|\leq\delta_{2}\}\right]}+\langle|\varphi_{x}|\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}+\delta_{2}.

Since
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\{|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|\leq\delta_{2}\}\cup\{|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y}|\leq\delta_{2}\}\right]
tends to
0
uniformly in
\Lambda
as
\delta_{2}
goes to
0
, and
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}
is regular, we can tune the values of
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
so that

\langle|\varphi_{x}|\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\leq\langle|\varphi_{x}|\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}+\varepsilon
, as desired. This completes the proof of the claim.

Since we have

\mathrm{C}:=\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\mathfrak{p}}-\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\eta}\geq 0

for every
\eta
such that
|\eta|\leq\mathfrak{p}
outside
\Lambda
by the Ginibre inequality,
it suffices to obtain an upper bound for
\mathrm{C}

which converges to
0
uniformly in
\eta\in A_{\Lambda}
as
\Lambda\to V
and
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Now let

\Phi^{\mathfrak{p}}=\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{z_{i}})^{2a_{i}}
and

\Phi^{\eta}=\varphi^{\eta}_{x}\varphi^{\eta}_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\varphi^{\eta}_{z_{i}})^{2a_{i}}.

Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}=\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\Phi^{\mathfrak{p}}-\Phi^{\eta}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(\Phi^{\mathfrak{p}}-\Phi^{\eta}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{B}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(\Phi^{\mathfrak{p}}-\Phi^{\eta}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{B^{c}}\right],

(2.9)

where
B
is the event
\left\{|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{w}|\leq|\varphi^{\eta}_{w}|+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\text{ for every }w\in\{x,y,z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\}\right\}
. Letting

f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y):=\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\text{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x})\text{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{y})\mid|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}|,|\varphi^{\eta}|\right],

and

f^{\eta}(x,y):=\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\text{sgn}(\varphi^{\eta}_{x})\text{sgn}(\varphi^{\eta}_{y})\mid|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}|,|\varphi^{\eta}|\right],

we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(\Phi^{\mathfrak{p}}-\Phi^{\eta}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{B}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[|\Phi^{\eta}|\left(f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y)-f^{\eta}(x,y)\right)\right]+O(\varepsilon^{1/4})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[(\Phi^{\eta})^{2}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y)-f^{\eta}(x,y)\right)^{2}\right]}+O(\varepsilon^{1/4})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[(\Phi^{\eta})^{2}\right]}\sqrt{2\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y)-f^{\eta}(x,y)\right]}+O(\varepsilon^{1/4}),

where the constant in the
O(\varepsilon^{1/4})
term is uniform in
\Lambda
. In the first line, we used the definition of the event
B
, and the fact that
f^{\mathfrak{p}}(y,z)\geq f^{\eta}(y,z)
to remove
\mathbbm{1}_{B}
. In the second line, we used the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and in the third line we used that
0\leq f^{\mathfrak{p}}(y,z)-f^{\eta}(y,z)\leq 2
.

Now, define
T
to be the event
\left\{|\varphi^{\eta}_{x}\varphi^{\eta}_{y}|\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right\}
, and note that

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\mathfrak{p}}-\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\eta}\geq\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[|\varphi^{\eta}_{x}\varphi^{\eta}_{y}|\left(f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y)-f^{\eta}(x,y)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[|\varphi^{\eta}_{x}\varphi^{\eta}_{y}|\left(f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y)-f^{\eta}(x,y)\right)\mathbbm{1}_{T}\right]\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y)-f^{\eta}(x,y)\right)\mathbbm{1}_{T}\right].

In the second line, we used that
|\varphi_{x}^{\mathfrak{p}}\varphi_{y}^{\mathfrak{p}}|\geq|\varphi_{x}^{\eta}\varphi_{y}^{\eta}|
and that
f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y)\geq 0
, and in the third line, we used that
f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y)\geq f^{\eta}(x,y)
. It follows that

\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y)-f^{\eta}(x,y)\right)\mathbbm{1}_{T}\right]\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

On the other hand,

\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(f^{\mathfrak{p}}(x,y)-f^{\eta}(x,y)\right)\mathbbm{1}_{T^{c}}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}[T^{c}],

and the right-hand side converges to
0
uniformly in
\Lambda
and
\eta
as
\varepsilon
goes to
0
. Since
\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[(\Phi^{\eta})^{2}\right]
remains bounded uniformly in
\eta
by regularity of
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}
, we can first send
\Lambda
to
V
and then
\varepsilon
to
0
to obtain that
\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(\Phi^{\mathfrak{p}}-\Phi^{\eta}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{B}\right]
converges to
0
uniformly in
\eta
.

To handle
\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(\Phi^{\mathfrak{p}}-\Phi^{\eta}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{B^{c}}\right]
, note that

\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(\Phi^{\mathfrak{p}}-\Phi^{\eta}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{B^{c}}\right]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(\Phi^{\mathfrak{p}}-\Phi^{\eta}\right)^{2}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}[B^{c}]}

by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. The first term in the right-hand side is bounded uniformly in
\Lambda
. For the second term, using a union bound we get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}[B^{c}]\leq\sum_{w\in\{x,y,z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\}}\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{w}|>|\varphi^{\eta}_{w}|+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right]\leq(k+2)\sqrt{\varepsilon},

where we used Markov’s inequality on the second line, together with the inequalities
\langle|\varphi_{w}|\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\leq\langle|\varphi_{w}|\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}+\varepsilon
and
|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{w}|\geq|\varphi^{\eta}_{w}|
. Taking first the limit as
\Lambda\uparrow V
and then
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, we can now conclude that
\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\left(\Phi^{\mathfrak{p}}-\Phi^{\eta}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{B^{c}}\right]
converges to
0
, which implies that

\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}^{+}=\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta},

as desired.

We now prove
3\Rightarrow 2
. Notice that it is sufficient
5
5

5

Indeed, if this holds, going to Laplace transforms, one finds that any finite (even) dimensional vector
(\varphi_{x_{1}},\ldots,\varphi_{x_{2k}})
has the same law under the two measures which in turns yields that the measures
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{+}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}
coincide on the set of even measurable functions.
to prove that for any
A:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
finitely supported with
\sum_{x\in V}A_{x}
even, one has

\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\beta}^{+}=\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\beta}.

First, we prove that for any
x,y\in V
, any
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
with
k\geq 0
, one has,

\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}^{+}=\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}.

(2.10)

Let us start with the case where
x
and
y
are such that there exists
z\in V
such that
J_{x,z}J_{z,y}>0
. Apply Ginibre’s inequality to
A=\mathbbm{1}_{x}+\mathbbm{1}_{y}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}2a_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{z_{i}}
and
B=\mathbbm{1}_{z}+\mathbbm{1}_{y}
, and use the hypothesis to get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{z}\varphi_{y}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}^{+}-\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{z}\varphi_{y}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle^{+}_{\beta}\langle\varphi_{z}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta}-\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}\langle\varphi_{z}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}\right|,

so that
\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}^{+}=\left\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}
.
To get the result in the general case, we use the irreducibility
(\textbf{C3})
of
J
and proceed inductively on the number
p
such that there exists
(x_{i})_{0\leq i\leq p}
with
x_{0}=x
,
x_{p}=y
, and
J_{x_{0},x_{1}}\ldots J_{x_{p-1},x_{p}}>0
. Notice that (
2.10
) also holds with
x=y
.

To conclude, it is sufficient to prove that for any
x_{1},\ldots,x_{2p},z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\in V
with
p\geq 1
and
k\geq 0
, and any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, one has,

\left\langle\varphi_{x_{1}}\ldots\varphi_{x_{2p}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}^{+}=\left\langle\varphi_{x_{1}}\ldots\varphi_{x_{2p}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{z_{i}}^{2a_{i}}\right\rangle_{\beta}.

Once again, let us proceed inductively on
p
. The case
p=1
was proved above. Assume the result holds for any collection of
2p-2
points with
p\geq 1
and consider
x_{1},\ldots,x_{2p}\in V
with
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\in V
and
k\geq 0
. Apply Ginibre’s inequality to
A=\sum_{i=1}^{2p}\mathbbm{1}_{x_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}2a_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{z_{i}}
and
B=\mathbbm{1}_{x_{2p-1}}+\mathbbm{1}_{x_{2p}}
to get the result.

We now prove
2\Rightarrow 1
. Let
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta)
. We can assume that
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}
is extremal. Define the measure
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\textup{sym}}_{\beta}
as follows: for any bounded measurable fonction
X:\mathbb{R}^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\langle X(\varphi)\rangle^{\textup{sym}}_{\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\langle X(\varphi)\rangle_{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\langle X(-\varphi)\rangle_{\beta}.

Then, notice that for any odd function
X
, one has
\langle X(\varphi)\rangle^{\textup{sym}}_{\beta}=\langle X(\varphi)\rangle^{0}_{\beta}=0
. If
X
is even, then
\langle X(\varphi)\rangle^{\textup{sym}}_{\beta}=\langle X(\varphi)\rangle^{0}_{\beta}
by the hypothesis, hence
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\textup{sym}}_{\beta}=\langle\cdot\rangle^{0}_{\beta}
. Now, the hypothesis applied to
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{0}
yields the relation
\langle\cdot\rangle^{0}_{\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\cdot\rangle^{-}_{\beta}
. The uniqueness of the decomposition into extremal states then allows to conclude that
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}\in\{\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta},\langle\cdot\rangle^{-}_{\beta}\}
.
∎

3
Switching principle for random tangled currents

In this section, we develop a random tangled current representation for
\varphi^{4}
which is analogous to the classical random current representation for Ising models. In comparison to the latter representation, an additional complexity appears through the notion of tanglings which we define below. When clear from context, we sometimes write
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}=\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}^{0}
to denote the free measure at
(\beta,h)
.

3.1
Current expansion

Let
G=(V,E)
be a countably infinite, locally finite graph. Let
\Lambda
be a finite subset of
V
.
To include boundary conditions, we introduce a ghost vertex
\mathfrak{g}
with the condition
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\equiv 1
and extend the model on the augmented set
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}=\Lambda\cup\{\mathfrak{g}\}
. Let
\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}:=\mathbb{N}^{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})}
be the space of currents on
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}
, where
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
. For
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
and
\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}
, we use the following notations to denote the value of
\mathbf{n}
on the pair
\{x,y\}
:
\mathbf{n}(\{x,y\})=\mathbf{n}_{x,y}=\mathbf{n}(x,y)
.

Given
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
, let

\partial\mathbf{n}:=\{x\in\Lambda:\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)\text{ is odd}\}
be the set of sources of
\mathbf{n}
, where

\Delta\mathbf{n}(x):=\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathbf{n}_{x,y}

is the
\mathbf{n}
-degree of
x
. Additionally, let
|\mathbf{n}|
denote the
L^{1}
norm of
\mathbf{n}
, i.e.

|\mathbf{n}|=\sum_{\{x,y\}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})}\mathbf{n}_{x,y}.

Let

\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}):=\Big\{A\in\mathbb{N}^{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}:\>A_{\mathfrak{g}}\leq 1,\>\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}A_{x}\text{ is even}\Big\}

be the set of admissible moments on
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Given
A,B\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, define
A+B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
by

(A+B)_{x}:=A_{x}+B_{x}

for all
x\in\Lambda
, and

(A+B)_{\mathfrak{g}}:=A_{\mathfrak{g}}+B_{\mathfrak{g}}\mod 2.

Given
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
, denote by
\partial A:=\{x\in\Lambda:\>A_{x}\text{ is odd}\}
its set of sources (note
\mathfrak{g}
is not included by convention). We denote by
A=\emptyset
the element of
\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
that satisfies
A_{x}=0
for all
x\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The above definitions also apply naturally to
\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
, the set of admissible moments on
\Lambda
.

In the following proposition, we obtain an expression for the spin correlations in terms of currents, and as a corollary we obtain the first Griffiths’ inequality. We recall that for
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
,
\varphi_{A}=\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\varphi_{x}^{A_{x}}
.

Proposition 3.1

(Current expansion for
\varphi^{4}
)
.

Let
\beta>0
and
h=(h_{x})_{x\in\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
. For any
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
,

\left\langle\varphi_{A}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}=\dfrac{\sum_{\partial\mathbf{n}=\partial A}w_{\beta,h}^{A}(\mathbf{n})}{\sum_{\partial\mathbf{n}=\emptyset}w_{\beta,h}^{\emptyset}(\mathbf{n})},

(3.1)

where

w_{\beta,h}^{A}(\mathbf{n}):=\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\dfrac{(\beta J_{x,y})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\dfrac{(\beta h_{x})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}!}\left\langle\varphi^{\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)+A_{x}}\right\rangle_{0},

(3.2)

and we recall that
\langle\cdot\rangle_{0}
is integration with respect to the single-site measure
\rho_{g,a}
.

In particular, Griffiths’ first inequality holds: if
h_{x}\geq 0
for every
x\in\Lambda
, then

\left\langle\varphi_{A}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}\geq 0.

Proof.

Expanding the partition function of the
\varphi^{4}
model in the same fashion as for the Ising model, we obtain the current representation of the
\varphi^{4}
model. More precisely,

\displaystyle Z_{\Lambda,\beta,h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\exp\left(\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}J_{x,y}\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}+\beta\sum_{x\in\Lambda}h_{x}\varphi_{x}\right)\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\rho_{g,a}(\text{d}\varphi_{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}}\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\dfrac{(\beta J_{x,y})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\dfrac{(\beta h_{x})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\varphi_{x}^{\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\rho_{g,a}(\text{d}\varphi_{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}}\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\dfrac{(\beta J_{x,y})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\dfrac{(\beta h_{x})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\rho_{g,a}(\text{d}\varphi).

Now, note that for
z\in\partial\mathbf{n}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\rho_{g,a}(\text{d}\varphi)=0,

since
\text{d}\rho_{g,a}
is an even measure.
Then,

Z_{\Lambda,\beta,h}=z_{g,a}^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}\\
\partial\mathbf{n}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\dfrac{(\beta J_{x,y})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\dfrac{(\beta h_{x})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}!}\left\langle\varphi^{\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\right\rangle_{0}.

By the same argument as above, we see that for
A\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{g}}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\varphi_{A}\exp\left(\beta\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}J_{x,y}\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}+\beta\sum_{x\in\Lambda}h_{x}\varphi_{x}\right)\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\rho_{g,a}(\text{d}\varphi_{x})=z_{g,a}^{|\Lambda|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}\\
\partial\mathbf{n}=\partial A\end{subarray}}w_{\beta,h}^{A}(\mathbf{n}).

∎

Remark 3.2
.

Note that we can also obtain a current expansion for
\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}^{\eta}
by viewing the boundary condition as an external field
h^{\prime}_{x}:=\sum_{y\notin\Lambda}J_{x,y}\eta_{y}
and then writing
\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}^{\eta}=\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h+h^{\prime}}^{0}
.

Remark 3.3
.

Due to presence of the moments
\langle\varphi^{\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\rangle_{0}
