, all known proofs of the classical switching lemma for the Ising model do not apply to the above current weights. In order to recover a switching principle, we need the notion of tangled currents, which is introduced in the next section.

Remark 3.4
.

When
h\equiv 0
, we let
w^{A}_{\beta}:=w^{A}_{\beta,0}
.
We may drop the
h
or
\beta
dependency on
w_{\beta,h}^{A}
when clear from context.

3.2
Tangled currents

In this section, we introduce the notion of tanglings for single and double currents. Let us start with the former case.

Single tangled current.

We fix
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
, and let
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
be such that
\partial\mathbf{n}=\partial A
. For each
z\in\Lambda
, we define the block
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
as follows : for each
y\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}
, it contains
\mathbf{n}_{z,y}
points labelled
(zy(k))_{1\leq k\leq\mathbf{n}_{z,y}}
, and
A_{z}
points labelled
(za(k))_{1\leq k\leq A_{z}}
. Note that
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
has cardinality
\Delta\mathbf{n}(z)+A_{z}
, which is an even number. We also write
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{A}(\mathbf{n})=\{\mathfrak{g}\}
, and when
A_{\mathfrak{g}}=1
we may also write
\mathfrak{g}a(1)=\mathfrak{g}
. For each
z\in\Lambda
, let
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}(z)
be the set of
even partitions
of the block
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
, i.e. for every
P=\{P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{k}\}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}(z)
, each
|P_{i}|
is even.
Define

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}:=\underset{z\in\Lambda}{\bigotimes}\,\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}(z).

An element
\mathpzc{t}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}
is called a
tangling
. We sometimes refer to an element
\mathpzc{t}_{z}
of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}(z)
as a tangling (of
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
). This will be clear from the context. We call the pair
(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})
a
tangled current
.

Let
\mathcal{H}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
be the graph consisting of vertex set

\bigcup_{z\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A}(\mathbf{n})

and edge set

\left(\bigcup_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\left\{\{xy(k),yx(k)\}:\>1\leq k\leq\mathbf{n}_{x,y}\right\}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{x\in\Lambda}\{\{x\mathfrak{g}(k),\mathfrak{g}\}:\>1\leq k\leq\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}\}\right).

Given any tangling
\mathpzc{t}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}
, the graph
\mathcal{H}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
naturally induces a multigraph
\mathcal{H}^{A}(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})
defined as follows. For each block
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
, if
\mathpzc{t}_{z}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}\}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}(z)
, for every
1\leq i\leq k
, we add
\binom{|P_{i}|}{2}
edges connecting pairwise the vertices lying in
P_{i}
. In words, we add to
\mathcal{H}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
the complete graph on the elements of each partition induced by
\mathpzc{t}
.
This gives a canonical notion of connectivity in a tangled current
(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})
. See Figure
1
for an illustration.

Remark 3.5
.

In the case
A=\emptyset
, we write
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})
instead of
\mathcal{H}^{\emptyset}(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})
.

Remark 3.6
.

We can also extend the definitions for
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}
and
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
by simply noticing that
\mathbf{n}
can be seen as an element of
\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
and
A
can be seen as an element of
\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
. For a tangling
\mathpzc{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{A}_{\mathbf{n}}
, the graph
\mathcal{H}^{A}(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})
is obtained via the same procedure as above, removing
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
.

Remark 3.7
.

Above, we connected all the elements in a given partition
P_{i}
by adding to
\mathcal{H}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
a complete graph on
P_{i}
. This choice is completely arbitrary and we could have chosen any connected spanning subgraph of
P_{i}
. However, we find that our choice is the most convenient solution as it preserves a fundamental property: in the graph
\mathcal{H}^{A}(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})
, the only vertices of odd degree are the ones labelled by
A
. As a consequence,
\mathcal{H}^{A}(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})
must connect these elements pairwise.

In the perspective of constructing a switching principle for tangled currents, it is natural to extend the above definitions to the case where
\mathbf{n}
is replaced by the sum of two currents.

Figure 1:
On the left: An example of a single tangled current multigraph
\mathcal{H}^{A}(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})
with
A_{x_{1}}=0
,
A_{x_{2}}=2
,
A_{x_{3}}=1
and
A_{x_{4}}=3
. The circles represent the blocks
\mathcal{B}^{A}(\mathbf{n})
; the small ellipses inside represent the elements of the partitions
\mathpzc{t}
. The vertices
\{za(k):k\geq 1,\>z\in\Lambda\}
are represented with squares. Notice that
x_{3},x_{4}
are sources of
\mathbf{n}
. On the right: An example of a double tangled current multigraph
\mathcal{H}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
. The vertices in blue are labelled by
(\mathbf{n}_{1},A)
and the ones in red by
(\mathbf{n}_{2},B)
. In this example,
A_{x_{1}}=A_{x_{2}}=A_{x_{3}}=0
and
A_{x_{4}}=2
; and
B_{x_{1}}=0
,
B_{x_{2}}=2
, and
B_{x_{3}}=B_{x_{4}}=1
.

Double tangled current.

Let
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda
be two finite subsets of
V
.
Fix
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
and
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{g}})
. Let
\mathbf{n}_{1}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
and
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
satisfy
\partial\mathbf{n}_{1}=\partial A
and
\partial\mathbf{n}_{2}=\partial B
. We trivially view
\mathbf{n}_{2}
as an element of
\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
by setting
\mathbf{n}_{2}(x,y)=0
for all pairs
\{x,y\}
with
x\in\Lambda
or
y\in\Lambda
. Additionally, we view
B
as an element of
\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
. To define a notion of tangled currents for the pair
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})
, we use a similar procedure as above. We define
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})
as the disjoint union of
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A}(\mathbf{n}_{1})
and
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{B}(\mathbf{n}_{2})
.
We write
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}(z)
for the set of even partitions of the set
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})
whose restriction to
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A}(\mathbf{n}_{1})
and
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{B}(\mathbf{n}_{2})
is also an even partition, i.e. for every
P=\{P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{k}\}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}(z)
, both
|P_{i}\cap\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A}(\mathbf{n}_{1})|
and
|P_{i}\cap\mathcal{B}_{z}^{B}(\mathbf{n}_{2})|
are even numbers. The elements of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}(z)
are called
admissible
partitions.
As above, we define

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}:=\underset{z\in\Lambda}{\bigotimes}\,\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}(z).

As above, we may define a graph
\mathcal{H}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})
.
For any tangling
\mathpzc{t}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}
, we obtain a multigraph
\mathcal{H}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
by the following procedure: as for the single tangled current, for each block
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})
, if
\mathpzc{t}_{z}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}\}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}(z)
, for every
1\leq i\leq k
, we add
\binom{|P_{i}|}{2}
edges connecting pairwise the vertices lying in
P_{i}
. This provides a natural notion of connectivity in
\mathcal{H}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})
. We let
\mathcal{H}^{A,B}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
be the induced subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
with vertex set all the vertices in blocks labelled by elements of
\Lambda^{\prime}
.

Finally, we introduce some useful terminology. Recall that each block
\mathcal{B}_{z}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})
contains four types of vertices: the
internal vertices
which are either labelled by
A
or by
B
, and the
external vertices
which are labelled either by
\mathbf{n}_{1}
or
\mathbf{n}_{2}
. For the switching principle stated in the next section, the relevant geometric object to consider is the graph
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
) obtained from
\mathcal{H}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
(resp.
\mathcal{H}^{A,B}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
) by removing the labels of all the external vertices.

Remark 3.8
.

As in Remark
3.6
, we can obtain a notion of tangled currents for pairs of currents
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})
such that
\mathbf{n}_{1}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
and
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}
, or
\mathbf{n}_{1}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}
and
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
. Moreover, if
A=B=\emptyset
, we write
B_{z}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})=B_{z}^{\emptyset,\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})
and
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})=\mathcal{H}^{\emptyset,\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})=\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\emptyset,\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
).

The space of (unlabelled) double tangled currents.

For
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
and
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
, we define
\Omega_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A,B}
to be the set of double tangled currents on
\Lambda
with source functions
A,B
, i.e the set of triples
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
where:
(\partial\mathbf{n}_{1},\partial\mathbf{n}_{2})=(\partial A,\partial B)
, and
\mathpzc{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{A,B}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}
. We sometimes identify such a triple with its associated multigraph
\mathcal{H}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
. We set

\Omega^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}:=\bigcup_{(A,B)\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)\times\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})}\Omega_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A,B}.

(3.3)

Moreover, we define
\overline{\Omega}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A,B}
to be the image of
\Omega_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A,B}
under the map
\mathcal{H}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})\mapsto\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
.

Remark 3.9
.

Note that there exists a (natural) bijection between
\overline{\Omega}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A,B}
and
\overline{\Omega}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A+B,\emptyset}
which can be described as follows.
If
\mathcal{H}\in\overline{\Omega}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A+B,\emptyset}
, each block
\mathcal{B}_{z}
of
\mathcal{H}
contains
A_{z}+B_{z}
internal vertices, declare that the first
A_{z}
internal vertices are labelled by
A
and that the last
B_{z}
internal vertices are labelled by
B
. This defines uniquely an element of
\overline{\Omega}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A,B}
that we denote by
\iota(\mathcal{H})
. It is not hard to construct the inverse of
\iota
.
If
F:\overline{\Omega}^{\mathcal{T},A,B}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we will often abuse notation and write
F=F\circ\iota
, which allows us to view
F
as a function on
\overline{\Omega}^{\mathcal{T},A+B,\emptyset}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}
as well.

3.3
A switching principle for tangled currents

Let
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda
be finite subsets of
V\cup\{\mathfrak{g}\}
. Fix
\beta>0
and
h=(h_{x})_{x\in V}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{V}
. Before stating the main result of the section, we need a definition.

Definition 3.10

(Pairing event)
.

Let
C\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
and
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
. Assume that
B\leq C
, i.e.
B_{x}\leq C_{x}
for every
x\in\Lambda^{\prime}
. Define
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{C}(B)
to be the subset of
\Omega_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},C,\emptyset}
consisting of all double tangled currents
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
satisfying the following condition: each connected component of
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{C,\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
intersects
\{zc(k):z\in\Lambda^{\prime},\>C_{z}-B_{z}+1\leq k\leq C_{z}\}
an even number of times.
Notice that
\mathcal{F}^{C}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(B)
only depends on
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{C,\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})
.
When
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda
, we write
\mathcal{F}^{C}(B)=\mathcal{F}^{C}_{\Lambda}(B)
.

Remark 3.11
.

The above definition is to be compared with the pairing event which naturally appears in the switching lemma of the Ising model.

Theorem 3.12
.

For every
z\in\Lambda
, every
(C,D)\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)\times\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
, every
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}\times\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
with
(\partial\mathbf{n}_{1},\partial\mathbf{n}_{2})=(\partial C,\partial D)
, there exists a probability measure
\rho_{z,\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{C,D}
on
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{C,D}(z)
such that the following holds.
Let
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
and
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
.
For every bounded
F:\overline{\Omega}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A,B}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\partial\mathbf{n}_{1}=\partial A\\
\partial\mathbf{n}_{2}=\partial B\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle w_{\beta,h}^{A}(\mathbf{n}_{1})w_{\beta,h}^{B}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\rho_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}\big[F[\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})]\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\partial\mathbf{n}_{1}=\partial(A+B)\\
\partial\mathbf{n}_{2}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}w_{\beta,h}^{A+B}(\mathbf{n}_{1})w_{\beta,h}^{\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\rho_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A+B,\emptyset}\big[F[\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A+B,\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})]\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{A+B}(B)}\big],

where

\rho_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}:=\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}\rho_{z,\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B},\quad\text{ and }\quad\rho_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A+B,\emptyset}:=\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}\rho_{z,\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A+B,\emptyset},

are measures on
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A+B,\emptyset}
respectively, and
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{A+B}(B)
is the event defined above.

Proof.

See Section
3.5
. ∎

Remark 3.13
.

As we will see in the proof, for any
z
, the measures
\rho_{z,\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}
only depend on
(g,a,\Delta\mathbf{n}_{1}(z),\Delta\mathbf{n}_{2}(z),A_{z},B_{z})
, and not on
\Lambda
,
\beta
,
h
or the rest of
\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}
.

We can also state the switching lemma in a probabilistic way. We start by introducing the random tangled current measures of interest. We define
\rho_{z,\mathbf{n}}^{A}:=\rho^{A,\emptyset}_{z,\mathbf{n},0}
and

\rho_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}:=\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}\rho_{z,\mathbf{n}}^{A}.

The measure
\rho_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}
can be viewed as a measure on single tangled currents.

With a small abuse of notation, we view the set of single tangled currents on
\Lambda
as a subset of
\Omega^{\mathcal{T}}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}
that we denote by
\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{T}}
. Similarly, we define for
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
the set
\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{T},A}\subset\Omega_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A,\emptyset}
.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda
be finite subsets of
V\cup\{\mathfrak{g}\}
. Let
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
and
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
. We define a measure
\mathbf{P}^{A}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}
on the
\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{T},A}
: for any
(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{T},A}
,

\mathbf{P}^{A}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}[(\mathbf{n},\mathpzc{t})]=\frac{w_{\beta,h}^{A}(\mathbf{n})\rho_{\mathbf{n}}^{A}[\mathpzc{t}]}{\sum_{\partial\mathbf{m}=\partial A}w_{\beta,h}^{A}(\mathbf{m})}.

We also define a measure
\mathbf{P}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime},\beta,h}^{A,B}
on
\Omega^{\mathcal{T},A,B}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}
: for
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})\in\Omega^{\mathcal{T},A,B}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}
,

\mathbf{P}^{A,B}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime},\beta,h}[(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})]=\frac{w_{\beta,h}^{A}(\mathbf{n}_{1})w_{\beta,h}^{B}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\rho^{A,B}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}[\mathpzc{t}]}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\partial\mathbf{m}_{1}=\partial A\\
\partial\mathbf{m}_{2}=\partial B\end{subarray}}w_{\beta,h}^{A}(\mathbf{m}_{1})w_{\beta,h}^{B}(\mathbf{m}_{2})}.

We write
\mathbf{E}^{A}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}
(resp.
\mathbf{E}^{A,B}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime},\beta,h}
) the expectation with respect to the measure
\mathbf{P}^{A}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}
(resp.
\mathbf{P}^{A,B}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime},\beta,h}
).

Using these definitions, we can reformulate the switching lemma in a more probabilistic way.

Corollary 3.15

(Probabilistic version of the switching lemma)
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
,
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
. For any bounded
F:\overline{\Omega}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T},A,B}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\frac{\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda^{\prime},\beta,h}}{\langle\varphi_{A+B}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}}\mathbf{E}^{A,B}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime},\beta,h}[F[\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})]]=\mathbf{E}^{A+B,\emptyset}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime},\beta,h}[F[\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A+B,\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})]\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{A+B}(B)}].

In what follows,
\beta,h
are often fixed and omitted to simplify the notation. In particular, we omit them in the notations of the tangling measures.

Remark 3.16
.

Taking
a>0
and letting
g\rightarrow 0
, the
\varphi^{4}
model on a finite graph converges to a (massive) Gaussian Free Field. It is therefore natural to expect the switching principle to pass through the limit. This is indeed the case, and one may additionally show that the tangling measures converge to the uniform measure on pairings, which is directly related to Wick’s formula.
Similarly, one may take the Ising limit as in (
1.1
) and ask a similar question. In this case, one can prove that the tangling measures converge to the Dirac measure supported on the trivial partition consisting of one element, thus recovering the standard switching lemma of the Ising model. We refer to
[
KPP24
, Section 4]
for more details.

3.4
Application: correlation inequalities

We fix
\beta>0
and drop it from the notation. The first result gives a new exact probabilistic representation for the ratio of spin correlations.

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite subset of
V
. Let
h=(h_{x})_{x\in\Lambda}\in(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\Lambda}
. For
A,B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
,

\dfrac{\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,h}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda,h}}{\langle\varphi_{A+B}\rangle_{\Lambda,h}}=\mathbf{P}_{\Lambda_{g},\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{A+B,\emptyset}[(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})\in\mathcal{F}^{A+B}(B)]

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Corollary
3.15
applied to
F=1
.
∎

We will now apply Proposition
3.17
to give new proofs of standard correlation inequalities for the
\varphi^{4}
model. Alternative proofs can be found in
[
GJ12
, Chapter 4]
.

Proposition 3.18

(Second Griffiths’ inequality)
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite subset of
V
, and
h=(h_{x})_{x\in\Lambda}\in(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\Lambda}
. Let
A,B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
. Then

\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda,h}\geq\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,h}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda,h}.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Proposition
3.17

∎

Proposition 3.19

(Monotonicity in volume)
.

Let
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda
be finite subsets of
V
, and
h=(h_{x})_{x\in\Lambda}\in(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\Lambda}
. Let
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
. Then,

\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda,h}\geq\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda^{\prime},h}.

Proof.

Apply Corollary
3.15
to
A=0
and
F=1
to obtain that

\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda^{\prime},h}=\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda,h}\mathbf{P}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}},\Lambda^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{B,\emptyset}[\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{B}(B)].

∎

Proposition 3.20
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite subset of
V
,
h=(h_{x})_{x\in\Lambda}\in(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\Lambda}
, and
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
. Then,

(i
)

\beta\mapsto\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h,g,a}
is non-decreasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

(ii)

g\mapsto\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h,g,a}
is non-increasing on
\mathbb{R}
,

(iii)

a\mapsto\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h,g,a}
is non-increasing on
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Notice that

\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}\beta}\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h,g,a}=\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}J_{x,y}\langle\varphi_{A};\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h},

\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}g}\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h,g,a}=-\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\langle\varphi_{A};\varphi_{x}^{4}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h,g,a},

and

\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}a}\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h,g,a}=-\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\langle\varphi_{A};\varphi_{x}^{2}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h,g,a},

where we used the notation
\langle\varphi_{A};\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h,g,a}:=\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}-\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}
.
∎

Remark 3.21
.

Note that there is also monotonicity in
h
and
J
analogous to the statements in Proposition
3.20
. They follow by similar considerations.

3.5
Proof of switching principle

In this section, we use the Griffiths–Simon approximation
[
GS73
]
to construct a random tangled current representation of
\varphi^{4}
on a finite subset
\Lambda
of
V
which satisfies a switching principle. The prelimit is an Ising model on the product of
\Lambda
with the complete graph on
N
vertices. We use the switching principle of the random current representation of the Ising model to obtain the switching principle for
\varphi^{4}
by taking limits, as appropriate. Along the way, we identify the underlying geometric structures that persist in the scaling limit.

3.5.1
Griffiths–Simon approximation

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
\Lambda_{N}:=\Lambda\times\{1,\ldots,N\}
. For
x\in\Lambda
, define

B_{x,N}:=\{(x,i)\}_{1\leq i\leq N},

and set
B_{\mathfrak{g},N}:=\{\mathfrak{g}\}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{N}
be the graph with vertex set
V(\mathcal{G}_{N}):=\Lambda_{N}\cup\{\mathfrak{g}\}
and edge set

E(\mathcal{G}_{N}):=E^{\textup{int}}(\mathcal{G}_{N})\cup E^{\textup{ext}}(\mathcal{G}_{N}),

where

\displaystyle E^{\textup{int}}(\mathcal{G}_{N})

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{x\in\Lambda}\mathcal{P}_{2}(B_{x,N}),

\displaystyle E^{\textup{ext}}(\mathcal{G}_{N})

\displaystyle:=\Big\{\{u,v\}\text{ such that there exists }\{x,y\}\in E(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})\text{ with }u\in B_{x,N}\text{ and }v\in B_{y,N}\Big\},

and, above,
\mathcal{P}_{2}(B_{x,N})
denotes the set of pairs in
B_{x,N}
.

Remark 3.22
.

An edge of the form
\{(x,j),(x,k)\}\in E^{\textup{int}}
is called internal, whereas an edge of the form
\{(x,j),(y,k)\}\in E^{\textup{ext}}
is called external.

We now introduce the Griffiths–Simon model. Let
\tilde{g}=(12g)^{1/4}
and
\tilde{a}=2a{\tilde{g}}^{-2}
. Define the coupling constants

c_{N}:=\tilde{g}^{-1}N^{-3/4},\qquad d_{N}:=\frac{1}{N}\left(1-\frac{\tilde{a}}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

(3.4)

For
\beta>0
,
h\in(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\Lambda}
and
\sigma
in
\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda_{N}}
, define the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{h}_{\Lambda,\beta,N}(\sigma)

\displaystyle:=-\beta c_{N}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda\\
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\end{subarray}}J_{x,y}\sigma_{(x,j)}\sigma_{(y,k)}

\displaystyle-\beta c_{N}\sum_{(x,j)\in\Lambda_{N}}h_{x}\sigma_{(x,j)}-d_{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Lambda\\
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{(x,j)}\sigma_{(x,k)}.

Definition 3.23
.

Let
\beta>0
. Let
\mu^{h}_{\Lambda,N,\beta}
be the probability measure on
\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda_{N}}
defined for
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda_{N}}
by

\mu_{\Lambda,N,\beta}^{h}(\sigma)=\dfrac{\exp(-\mathbf{H}^{h}_{\Lambda,\beta,N}(\sigma))}{\mathbf{Z}(\Lambda,N,\beta,h)}

where
\mathbf{Z}(\Lambda,N,\beta,h)
is the partition function. In the specific case where
\Lambda
is reduced to a point
x
, we denote this measure
\mu_{x,N,\beta}^{h}
. Note that this measure does not depend on
x
.

Observe that
\mu^{h}_{\Lambda,N,\beta}
is an Ising model on
\Lambda_{N}
. The coupling constants are such that the model has a scaling limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
, which is the
\varphi^{4}
model on
\Lambda
with parameters
(g,a)
at
(\beta,h)
. The natural observable to state the convergence is the block-spin average, defined for
x\in\Lambda
by

\Phi_{x,N}:=c_{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma_{(x,j)}.

The following result was first derived in
[
GS73
]
to obtain, amongst other things, the Lee–Yang theorem for the
\varphi^{4}
model on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. It was later reproved for a larger class of models in
[
DN75
]
.

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
\beta>0
and
h\in(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\Lambda}
. Then, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}^{\Lambda}
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mu^{h}_{\Lambda,N,\beta}\left[\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\Phi_{x,N}^{p_{x}}\right]=\Big\langle\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\varphi_{x}^{p_{x}}\Big\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}.

Remark 3.25
.

Proposition
3.24
can be seen as a generalisation of the central limit theorem to a class of dependent random variables. The different rescaling factor
c_{N}\sim N^{-3/4}
from the usual
N^{-1/2}
scaling for independent random variables is intrinsically linked to the non-Gaussian limiting random variable. A more sophisticated viewpoint is that the coupling constants are chosen such that the
N\rightarrow\infty
limit corresponds to a near-critical scaling limit of the Curie–Weiss model. Thus, the convergence of moments of the block-spin magnetisations to the
\varphi^{4}
moments can be seen as a statement about the model lying in a
\varphi^{4}
universality class. Indeed, generalisations of these results leading to densities with respect to
\varphi^{2k}
measures, i.e. distinct universality classes, can be found in
[
EN78
]
.

We strengthen the above convergence result for the specific case of a single block-spin. More precisely, we show that in this instance we can neglect diagonal terms.

Lemma 3.26
.

For any
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\Lambda
,

(c_{N}N)^{p}\mu^{0}_{x,N,\beta}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{p}\sigma_{(x,j)}\right]\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\left\langle\varphi^{p}\right\rangle_{0}.

Proof.

For
p=1
, there is nothing to show because both the sequence and
\left\langle\varphi\right\rangle_{0}
are equal to
0
, so let us assume that
p\geq 2
. Let
N\geq p
. Expanding
\Phi_{x,N}^{p}
and taking cases according to whether some index repeats or not we obtain

\displaystyle\mu^{0}_{x,N,\beta}\big[\Phi_{x,N}^{p}\big]=c_{N}^{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{p})\in\{1,\ldots,N\}^{p}\\
\forall k\neq\ell,\>i_{k}\neq i_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\mu^{0}_{x,N,\beta}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{p}\sigma_{(x,i_{j})}\right]

\displaystyle+c_{N}^{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{p})\in\{1,\ldots,N\}^{p}\\
\exists k<\ell,\>i_{k}=i_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\mu^{0}_{x,N,\beta}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{p}\sigma_{(x,i_{j})}\right].

Note that

\displaystyle c_{N}^{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{p})\in\{1,\ldots,N\}^{p}\\
\forall k\neq\ell,\>i_{k}\neq i_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\mu^{0}_{x,N,\beta}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{p}\sigma_{(x,i_{j})}\right]

\displaystyle=c^{p}_{N}p!\binom{N}{p}\mu^{0}_{x,N,\beta}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{p}\sigma_{(x,j)}\right]

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))(c_{N}N)^{p}\mu^{0}_{x,N,\beta}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{p}\sigma_{(x,j)}\right]

by the symmetries of the complete graph, and

0\leq c_{N}^{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{p})\in\{1,\ldots,N\}^{p}\\
\exists k<\ell,\>i_{k}=i_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\mu^{0}_{x,N,\beta}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{p}\sigma_{(x,i_{j})}\right]\leq c_{N}^{2}{p\choose 2}N\mu^{0}_{x,N,\beta}\big[\Phi_{x,N}^{p-2}\big]\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

where we have used that each summand is non-negative by Griffiths’ inequality. The desired assertion then follows from Proposition
3.24
.
∎

3.5.2
A conditional proof

We now introduce the random current representation for
\mu^{h}_{\Lambda,N,\beta}
. For ease of notation, we adopt the following conventions. First, we write
\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{N}:=\Omega_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}
to denote the space of currents on
\mathcal{G}_{N}
. Second, for
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
,
x,y\in\Lambda
and
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we set

\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,j,y,k):=\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\big((x,j),(y,k)\big),\qquad\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,j,\mathfrak{g}):=\mathbf{n}\big((x,j),\mathfrak{g}\big).

The random current expansion for
\mu^{h}_{\Lambda,\beta,N}
takes the following form: for any
S\subset\Lambda_{N}
,

\mu_{\Lambda,N,\beta}^{h}[\sigma_{S}]=\frac{\sum_{\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=S}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})}{\sum_{\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\emptyset}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})},

where

\displaystyle w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda\\
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{(\beta c_{N}^{2}J_{x,y})^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,j,y,k)}}{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,j,y,k)!}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Lambda\\
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{(d_{N})^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,j,x,k)}}{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,j,x,k)!}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{(x,j)\in\Lambda_{N}}\dfrac{(\beta c_{N}h_{x})^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,j,\mathfrak{g})}}{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,j,\mathfrak{g})!}.

Let
\Theta_{N}:\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{N}\rightarrow\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
be the projection map defined,
for all
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{N}
and
\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}
, by

\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})_{\{x,y\}}:=\sum_{j,k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,j,y,k).

We then have that
\partial\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\partial A
, where
A
is defined for
x\in\Lambda
by
A_{x}=|\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\cap B_{x,N}|
.

Conversely, we can lift any
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
to a set of currents in
\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{N}
once we have fixed an injection of admissible moments
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
to subsets of
\Lambda_{N}
. For convenience, we fix the natural injection: for
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
, define

\tilde{A}:=\bigcup_{x\in\Lambda}\{(x,j):\>1\leq j\leq A_{x}\}.

Then, the lift of
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
with source set
\partial A
is the set of
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{N}
such that
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}
and
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}
.

Motivated by this observation and Lemma
3.26
, we define the renormalised weight for
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
by

\overline{w}_{N,A,\beta}(\mathbf{n}):=\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}),

where
|A|=\sum_{x\in\Lambda}A_{x}
and

\mathbf{Z}_{N}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega_{B_{o,N}}\\
\partial\mathbf{n}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}).

We emphasise that the renormalisation factor
c_{N}N\sim N^{1/4}
is directly linked to Proposition
3.24
.

An important step in the proof of the switching principle is that the renormalised weights converge to those of the
\varphi^{4}
current expansion. To prove this, we need to understand the geometric structure of the currents that persists in the limit. It consists of currents which belong to the set
W_{N}
defined below.

Definition 3.27
.

Let
W_{N}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})=W_{N}:=W_{1,N}\cap W_{2,N}
, where

\displaystyle W_{1,N}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}:\>\forall(x,j)\in\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\setminus\{\mathfrak{g}\},\>\forall e\in E^{\textup{ext}}\text{ with }(x,j)\in e,\>\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{e}=0\right\},

\displaystyle W_{2,N}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}:\>\forall(x,j)\in\Lambda_{N},\>\sum_{e\in E^{\textup{ext}}:\>(x,j)\in e}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{e}\leq 1\Big\}.

In words, the currents in
W_{1,N}
consists of those whose sources are separated from exterior block-spins by other internal vertices; whereas
W_{2,N}
consists of currents whose weight on external edges does not exceed
1
. We have the following result.

Proposition 3.28
.

For all
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
and
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
,

\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W_{N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}w_{\beta}^{A}(\mathbf{n}),

and

\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W_{N}^{c}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

(3.5)

Proof.

See Section
3.5.3
.
∎

Corollary 3.29
.

For all
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
and
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
,

\overline{w}_{N,A,\beta}(\mathbf{n})\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}w_{\beta}^{A}(\mathbf{n}).

Guided by this intuition and Proposition
3.28
, we isolate how these geometric structures intertwine in the statement of the usual switching lemma for
\mu^{h}_{\Lambda,\beta,N}
, which takes the following form.

In what follows we fix
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda
two finite subsets of
V\cup\{\mathfrak{g}\}
. This is slightly different from the convention we had above in which
\Lambda
was a subset of
V
.

Lemma 3.30
.

Let
S\subset\Lambda_{N}
,
T\subset\Lambda^{\prime}_{N}
, and let
F:\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a bounded measurable function. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=S,\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=T\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})F(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})

(3.6)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=S\Delta T,\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})F(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})\mathbbm{1}[\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\Lambda^{\prime}_{N}}],

where
\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\Lambda^{\prime}}
is the event that every cluster of the multigraph induced by
(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{N}}
intersects
T
an even number of times.

If we proceed naively in expressing the left-hand side and the right-hand side in terms of projected currents and renormalised weights, we encounter a homogeneity issue. Indeed, let
\mathbf{n}_{1}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}
, and
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
with source sets
\partial A,\partial B
associated to
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
, and
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
respectively. Suppose we fix injections such that
\tilde{A}
and
\tilde{B}
may intersect. Then, the left-hand side of the switching lemma has a scaling factor
(c_{N}N)^{|A|+|B|}
, whereas the right-hand side has a scaling factor
(c_{N}N)^{|\tilde{A}\Delta\tilde{B}|}
, which is strictly smaller.

To resolve this issue, we require a consistent convention (note that this choice is consistent with Remark
3.9
) for injecting the sources of fixed admissible moments,
A
and
B
, in such a way that their lifts
\tilde{A}
and
\tilde{B}
satisfy
\tilde{A}\cap\tilde{B}=\emptyset
. The natural such injection of the ordered pair
(A,B)
is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{A}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{x\in\Lambda}\{(x,j):1\leq j\leq A_{x}\},

\displaystyle\tilde{B}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{x\in\Lambda}\{(x,j):A_{x}+1\leq j\leq A_{x}+B_{x}\}.

Consider the lefthand side of (
3.6
) for
S=\widetilde{A}
and
T=\widetilde{B}
. By conditioning on the underlying projected current in
\Omega_{\Lambda}
or
\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
, and then splitting the sum over
W_{N}
and
W_{N}^{c}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\text{(LHS)}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\widetilde{A},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=\widetilde{B}\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})F(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{n}_{1}\in\Omega_{\Lambda},\>\partial\mathbf{n}_{1}=\partial A\\
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}},\>\partial\mathbf{n}_{2}=\partial B\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})=\mathbf{n}_{1},\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}_{2}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\widetilde{A},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=\widetilde{B}\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})F(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{n}_{1}\in\Omega_{\Lambda},\>\partial\mathbf{n}_{1}=\partial A\\
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}},\>\partial\mathbf{n}_{2}=\partial B\end{subarray}}(\Sigma_{1,N}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})+\Sigma_{2,N}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})),

where

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1,N}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in W_{N}(\Lambda),\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W_{N}(\Lambda^{\prime})\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})=\mathbf{n}_{1},\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}_{2}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\widetilde{A},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=\widetilde{B}\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})F(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}),

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2,N}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\notin W_{N}(\Lambda)\textup{ or }\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\notin W_{N}(\Lambda^{\prime})\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})=\mathbf{n}_{1},\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}_{2}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\widetilde{A},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=\widetilde{B}\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})F(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}).

As a consequence of Proposition
3.28
, we find that (with the proper scaling)
\Sigma_{2,N}
converges to
0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

Lemma 3.31
.

Let
F:\Omega_{\Lambda}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be bounded. Then, for any
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
,
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
and
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}\times\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
such that
(\partial\mathbf{n}_{1},\partial\mathbf{n}_{2})=(\partial A,\partial B)
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|+|B|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|+|\Lambda^{\prime}|}}\Sigma_{2,N}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})=0.

Proof.

Since
F
is bounded,

\left|\frac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|+|B|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{2|\Lambda|+|\Lambda^{\prime}|}}\Sigma_{2,N}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\right|\leq\|F\|_{\infty}\left((1)+(2)\right),

where

\displaystyle(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in W_{N}^{c}(\Lambda)\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})=\mathbf{n}_{1}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\widetilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})\frac{(c_{N}N)^{|B|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda^{\prime}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}_{2}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=\widetilde{B}\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}),

\displaystyle(2)

\displaystyle=\frac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})=\mathbf{n}_{1}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\widetilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})\frac{(c_{N}N)^{|B|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda^{\prime}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W_{N}^{c}(\Lambda^{\prime})\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}_{2}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=\widetilde{B}\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}).

The result then follows from rewriting the weights in terms of renormalised weights and applying Proposition
3.28
.
∎

The above lemma implies that it is sufficient to restrict our attention to pairs of currents that belong to
W_{N}(\Lambda)\times W_{N}(\Lambda^{\prime})
in the switching lemma of (
3.6
). In order to understand the geometry of the sum of two such currents, we further decompose into internal and external components.

Definition 3.32
.

Let

W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}(\Lambda)=\{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W_{2,N}:\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,i,x,j)=0\text{ for all $x\in\Lambda$}\}
, and

W^{\textup{int}}_{N}(\Lambda)=\{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{N}:\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(x,i,y,j)=0\text{ if }x\neq y\}
.

Note that any
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W_{N}(\Lambda)
admits the decomposition
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{\textup{ext}}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{\textup{int}}
with
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{\textup{ext}}\in W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}(\Lambda)
and
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{\textup{int}}\in W^{\textup{int}}_{N}(\Lambda)
such that
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{\textup{ext}}\cap\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\emptyset
and
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{\textup{int}}=\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{\textup{ext}}\cup\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}
. Furthermore, we can write
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{\textup{int}}=\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{\textup{int}}(x)
, where
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{\textup{int}}(x)\in W^{\textup{int}}_{x,N}(\Lambda)
for

W^{\textup{int}}_{x,N}(\Lambda)=\{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W^{\textup{int}}_{N}(\Lambda):\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(y,i,z,j)=0\text{ for all $(y,z)\neq(x,x)$}\}.

These definitions extend to
\Lambda^{\prime}
and since a current on
\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
can naturally be extended to a current on
\Omega_{\Lambda}
, one has that
W_{x,N}^{\textup{int}}(\Lambda^{\prime})=W_{x,N}^{\textup{int}}(\Lambda)
for all
x\in\Lambda^{\prime}
.

In order to identify the scaling limit of
\Sigma_{1,N}
, we have to encode how the connectivity properties inside the block-spins intertwine when summing over the internal components. We formalise this as follows.

Definition 3.33
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. For
S_{x},T_{x}\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
\tilde{S}_{x}:=\{(x,i):1\leq i\leq S_{x}\}\subset B_{x,N}
and
\tilde{T}_{x}:=\{(x,i):S_{x}+1\leq i\leq S_{x}+T_{x}\}
. Let
\pi_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x}}
be the measure on
\Omega_{B_{x,N}}
defined by

\pi_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x}}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{x}):=(c_{N}N)^{S_{x}}\,\frac{w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{x})\mathbbm{1}_{\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{x}=\tilde{S}_{x}}}{\underset{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{x}\in W^{\textup{int}}_{x,N},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{x}=\emptyset}{\sum}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{x})}.

Furthermore, denote by
\pi_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x},\tilde{T}_{x}}
the pushforward of the product measure
\pi_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x}}\otimes\pi_{x,N}^{\tilde{T}_{x}}
on
\Omega_{B_{x,N}}\times\Omega_{B_{x,N}}
by the mapping
(\mathbf{n}^{x},\mathbf{m}^{x})\mapsto\mathbf{n}^{x}+\mathbf{m}^{x}
.

Remark 3.34
.

Note that

\pi^{\tilde{S}_{x}}_{x,N}(\Omega_{B_{x,N}})=(c_{N}N)^{S_{x}}\mu^{0}_{x,N,\beta}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{S_{x}}\sigma_{(x,i)}\right].

Let
A_{x}\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the following equivalence relation on
\{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{x}\in\Omega_{B_{x,N}}:\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{x}=\tilde{A}_{x}\}
. We say that
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{x}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{x}
are equivalent, and write
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{x}\simeq\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{x}
, if for all
1\leq i,j\leq A_{x}
,

\{(x,i)\overset{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{x}}{\longleftrightarrow}(x,j)\}\Leftrightarrow\{(x,i)\overset{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{x}}{\longleftrightarrow}(x,j)\}.

Following the notations of Definition
3.33
, denote by
\tilde{\rho}_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x}}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x},\tilde{T}_{x}}
the induced measures corresponding to
\pi_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x}}
and
\pi_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x},\tilde{T}_{x}}
, respectively, on the quotient
\sigma
-algebra. Note that these measures are finite due to the finiteness of the underlying space. Let
\rho_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x}}
and
\rho_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x},\tilde{T}_{x}}
denote the corresponding probability measures, which can be identified as probability distribution on even partitions of
K_{S_{x}}
and
K_{S_{x}+T_{x}}
, respectively.

Lemma 3.35
.

Let
S_{x},T_{x}\in\mathbb{N}
. The two sequences of probability measures
(\rho_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x}})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\rho_{x,N}^{\tilde{S}_{x},\tilde{T}_{x}})
are tight. In both cases, the convergence along subsequences is pointwise on events. We denote their respective limits by
\rho_{x}^{\tilde{S}_{x}}
and
\rho_{x}^{\tilde{S}_{x},\tilde{T}_{x}}
.

Proof.

Since the measures considered here are defined on a finite space, it suffices to prove that the measure of the entire space converges in each case. But this is a direct consequence of Remark
3.34
and Lemma
3.26
.
∎

Definition 3.36
.

If
P,Q
are two partitions of a set
\mathcal{S}
, we say that
P
is coarser than
Q
, and write
P\succ Q
, if any element of
P
can be written as a union of elements of
Q
.

The following proposition is a direct consequence of the constructions above.

Proposition 3.37

(The measure
\rho^{\tilde{S}_{x},\tilde{T}_{x}}_{x}
stochastically dominates
\rho^{\tilde{S}_{x}}_{x}\sqcup\rho^{\tilde{T}_{x}}_{x}
)
.

Let
S_{x},T_{x}\in 2\mathbb{N}
.
There exists a coupling of
\rho^{\tilde{S}_{x},\tilde{T}_{x}}_{x}
,
\rho^{\tilde{S}_{x}}_{x}
and
\rho^{\tilde{T}_{x}}_{x}
such that if
(X,Y,Z)\sim(\rho^{\tilde{S}_{x},\tilde{T}_{x}}_{x},\rho^{\tilde{S}_{x}}_{x},\rho^{\tilde{T}_{x}}_{x})
and if
Y\sqcup Z
is the partition whose partition classes are the partition classes of
Y
and
Z
,
then

X\succ Y\sqcup Z,\text{almost surely.}

We write,

\rho^{\tilde{S}_{x},\tilde{T}_{x}}_{x}\succ\rho^{\tilde{S}_{x}}_{x}\sqcup\rho^{\tilde{T}_{x}}_{x}.

(3.7)

Definition 3.38
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
,
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
and let
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}\times\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
with
(\partial\mathbf{n}_{1},\partial\mathbf{n}_{2})=(\partial A,\partial B)
. Let
F:\Omega_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be bounded and measurable with respect to the quotient
\sigma
-algebra induced by the equivalence relation
\simeq
. We define

\tilde{\rho}^{A,B}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},N}[F]:=\frac{1}{|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)|}\Big(\sum_{(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})\in W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)}\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}\tilde{\rho}_{z,N}^{\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}}(z)\sqcup\widetilde{A_{z}},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}}(x)\sqcup\widetilde{B_{z}}}\Big)[F],

where

W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B):=\Bigg\{(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})\in W^{\textup{ext}}(\Lambda)\times W^{\textup{ext}}(\Lambda^{\prime}):\begin{array}[]{c}\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}})=\mathbf{n}_{1},\,\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})=\mathbf{n}_{2}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}}\cap\widetilde{A}=\emptyset,\,\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}}\cap\widetilde{B}=\emptyset\end{array}\Bigg\},

and we use the convention that
\tilde{A}_{z},\tilde{B}_{z}=\emptyset
if
A_{z},B_{z}=0
. We denote by
\rho_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},N}^{A,B}
the associated probability measures.

Lemma 3.39
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
,
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
and let
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}\times\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
with
(\partial\mathbf{n}_{1},\partial\mathbf{n}_{2})=(\partial A,\partial B)
. Then, the sequence of probability measures
(\rho_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},N}^{A,B})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight.

Proof.

As in Lemma
3.35
, this result is a consequence of the finiteness of the underlying probability space together with the convergence

\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},N}^{A,B}[1]\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\prod_{z\in\Lambda}\left\langle\varphi^{\Delta\mathbf{n}_{1}(z)+A_{z}}\right\rangle_{0}\left\langle\varphi^{\Delta\mathbf{n}_{2}(z)+B_{z}}\right\rangle_{0}.

∎

Corollary 3.40
.

There exists an increasing sequence
(N_{k})_{k\geq 1}
such that for any
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
,
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
, and for any
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}\times\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
satisfying
\partial\mathbf{n}_{1}=\partial A
and
\partial\mathbf{n}_{2}=\partial B
, the sequence of measures
(\rho_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},N_{k}}^{A,B})_{k\geq 1}
weakly converges to a measure
\rho^{A,B}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}
.

Remark 3.41
.

Although for our purposes subsequential limits suffice, identification of the full limit has recently been established
[
KPP24
]
.

The following estimate on the cardinality of
W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)
will be useful throughout this section.

Lemma 3.42
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
,
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
and let
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}\times\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
with
(\partial\mathbf{n}_{1},\partial\mathbf{n}_{2})=(\partial A,\partial B)
. Then we have

|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)|=(1+o(1))\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\frac{N^{2\mathbf{n}_{1}(x,y)+2\mathbf{n}_{2}(x,y)}}{\mathbf{n}_{1}(x,y)!\mathbf{n}_{2}(x,y)!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\frac{N^{\mathbf{n}_{1}(x,\mathfrak{g})+\mathbf{n}_{2}(x,\mathfrak{g})}}{\mathbf{n}_{1}(x,\mathfrak{g})!\mathbf{n}_{2}(x,\mathfrak{g})!}.

Proof.

It suffices to estimate the cardinality of
W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n};A):=W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n},0;A,0)
. To this end, we introduce the following procedure. We first enumerate the elements of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\Lambda)
. We will count inductively the number of lifts
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}
of
\mathbf{n}
which satisfy
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}
and
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\cap\tilde{A}=\emptyset
.

Starting from the first element
\{x,y\}
according to this ordering and taking into account that we cannot use any point in
\tilde{A}
, we see that we have

\binom{N-A_{x}}{\mathbf{n}(x,y)}\binom{N-A_{y}}{\mathbf{n}(x,y)}\mathbf{n}(x,y)!

choices for the piece of current between the blocks
B_{x,N}
and
B_{y,N}
when
\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda
, and

\binom{N-A_{x}}{\mathbf{n}(x,\mathfrak{g})}

choices if
x\in\Lambda
and
y=\mathfrak{g}
. We then proceed to the second element
\{z,w\}
. In that case, the only additional condition we need to take into account is that we have attributed
\mathbf{n}(x,y)
vertices of
B_{x,N}
and
B_{y,N}
in the preceding step that we cannot re-use (because the lifts we construct belong to
W_{2,N}
). If
z,w\in\Lambda
, then we have

\binom{N-A_{z}-\mathbbm{1}_{z\in\{x,y\}}\mathbf{n}(x,y)}{\mathbf{n}(z,w)}\binom{N-A_{w}-\mathbbm{1}_{w\in\{x,y\}}\mathbf{n}(x,y)}{\mathbf{n}(z,w)}\mathbf{n}(z,w)!

choices for the piece of lift between
x
and
z
. We can iterate this procedure to compute the number of suitable lifts of
\mathbf{n}
.

It follows from the above procedure that

\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\binom{N-\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)-A_{x}}{\mathbf{n}(x,y)}\binom{N-\Delta\mathbf{n}(y)-A_{y}}{\mathbf{n}(x,y)}\mathbf{n}(x,y)!\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\binom{N-\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)-A_{x}}{\mathbf{n}(x,\mathfrak{g})}\leq\\
|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n};A)|\leq\prod_{\{x,y\}\in\Lambda}\binom{N}{\mathbf{n}(x,y)}^{2}\mathbf{n}(x,y)!\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\binom{N}{\mathbf{n}(x,\mathfrak{g})},

hence

|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n};A)|=(1+o(1))\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\frac{N^{2\mathbf{n}(x,y)}}{\mathbf{n}(x,y)!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\frac{N^{\mathbf{n}(x,\mathfrak{g})}}{\mathbf{n}(x,\mathfrak{g})!},

(3.8)

as desired.
∎

In what follows, we fix a sequence
(N_{k})_{k\geq 1}
as in Corollary
3.40
.

Lemma 3.43
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
,
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
and let
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}\times\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
with
(\partial\mathbf{n}_{1},\partial\mathbf{n}_{2})=(\partial A,\partial B)
. Then, along the subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\geq 1}
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{(c_{N_{k}}N_{k})^{|A|+|B|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N_{k}}^{|\Lambda|+|\Lambda^{\prime}|}}\Sigma_{1,N_{k}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})=w_{\beta}^{A}(\mathbf{n}_{1})w_{\beta}^{B}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\rho^{A,B}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}[F].

Proof.

By definition

\frac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|+|B|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|+|\Lambda^{\prime}|}}\Sigma_{1,N}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})=\frac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|+|B|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|+|\Lambda^{\prime}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in W_{N}(\Lambda),\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W_{N}(\Lambda^{\prime})\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})=\mathbf{n}_{1},\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}_{2}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\widetilde{A},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=\widetilde{B}\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})F(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}).

For
i=1,2
, write
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{i}=\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\textup{ext}}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}_{i}}^{\textup{int}}
with
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\textup{ext}}\in W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}
satisfying
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\textup{ext}}\cap\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{i}=\emptyset
, and
\tilde{\mathbf{n}_{i}}^{\textup{int}}\in W^{\textup{int}}_{N}
. Then, if
\mathbf{n}_{1}^{\textup{e}xt},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{e}xt}\in W^{\textup{e}xt}_{N}
are such that
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{e}xt})=\mathbf{n}_{1},\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{e}xt})=\mathbf{n}_{2}
, one has

w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}})w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})=\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}(\beta c_{N}^{2}J_{x,y})^{\mathbf{n}_{1}(x,y)+\mathbf{n}_{2}(x,y)}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}(\beta c_{N}h_{x})^{\mathbf{n}_{1}(x,\mathfrak{g})+\mathbf{n}_{2}(x,\mathfrak{g})}.

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\frac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|+|B|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|+|\Lambda^{\prime}|}}\Sigma_{1,N}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})=\frac{|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)|}{(c_{N}N)^{|\mathbf{n}_{1}|+|\mathbf{n}_{2}|}}\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}(\beta c_{N}^{2}J_{x,y})^{\mathbf{n}_{1}(x,y)+\mathbf{n}_{2}(x,y)}

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{x\in\Lambda}(\beta c_{N}h_{x})^{\mathbf{n}_{1}(x,\mathfrak{g})+\mathbf{n}_{2}(x,\mathfrak{g})}\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},N}^{A,B}[F].

Recall now Lemma
3.42
and that

\tilde{\rho}^{A,B}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}[1]=\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle\varphi_{x}^{\Delta\mathbf{n}_{1}(x)+A_{x}}\right\rangle_{0}\left\langle\varphi_{x}^{\Delta\mathbf{n}_{2}(x)+B_{x}}\right\rangle_{0}.

The desired convergence along the subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\geq 1}
follows readily.
∎

Using the same method as above, we also obtain the following.

Lemma 3.44
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
,
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
and let
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}\times\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
with
(\partial\mathbf{n}_{1},\partial\mathbf{n}_{2})=(\partial A,\partial B)
. Then, along the subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\geq 1}
,

\frac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|+|B|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|+|\Lambda^{\prime}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in W_{N}(\Lambda),\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W_{N}(\Lambda^{\prime})\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})=\mathbf{n}_{1},\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}_{2}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\widetilde{A}\sqcup\widetilde{B},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})w_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})F(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\tilde{B}}^{\Lambda^{\prime}_{N}}}\\
\underset{}{\longrightarrow}w_{\beta}^{A+B}(\mathbf{n}_{1})w_{\beta}^{\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\rho^{A+B,\emptyset}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}[F[\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A+B,\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})]\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{A+B}(B)}],

(3.9)

where
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{A+B}(B)
is the event defined in Theorem
3.12
.

We are now equipped to complete the conditional proof of Theorem
3.12

Proof of Theorem
3.12
.

We first apply (
3.6
) for
F\mathbbm{1}_{C}
, where

C:=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\Delta\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}}(x,i)+\Delta\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}}(x,i))\leq M,\>\forall x\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}\right\}

for some
M\geq 1
. Note that
F\mathbbm{1}_{C}
is bounded. Using the two preceding lemmas and taking the limit along an appropriate sequence
(N_{k})_{k\geq 1}
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{n}_{1}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}:\partial\mathbf{n}_{1}=\partial A\\
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}:\partial\mathbf{n}_{2}=\partial B\end{subarray}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in C}w_{\beta}^{A}(\mathbf{n}_{1})w_{\beta}^{B}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\rho^{A,B}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}\big[F[\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})]\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{n}_{1}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}:\partial\mathbf{n}_{1}=\partial(A+B)\\
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}:\partial\mathbf{n}_{2}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in C}w_{\beta}^{A+B}(\mathbf{n}_{1})w_{\beta}^{\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\rho^{A+B,\emptyset}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}\big[F[\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A+B,\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})]\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{A+B}(B)}\big].

Sending
M
to infinity and using the dominated convergence theorem we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{n}_{1}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}:\partial\mathbf{n}_{1}=\partial A\\
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}:\partial\mathbf{n}_{2}=\partial B\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle w_{\beta}^{A}(\mathbf{n}_{1})w_{\beta}^{B}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\rho^{A,B}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}\big[F[\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A,B}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})]\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{n}_{1}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}:\partial\mathbf{n}_{1}=\partial(A+B)\\
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}:\partial\mathbf{n}_{2}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}w_{\beta}^{A+B}(\mathbf{n}_{1})w_{\beta}^{\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\rho^{A+B,\emptyset}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}\big[F[\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{A+B,\emptyset}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathpzc{t})]\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{A+B}(B)}\big].

It remains to prove that for
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
,
B\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\prime})
and
(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}\times\Omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
with
(\partial\mathbf{n}_{1},\partial\mathbf{n}_{2})=(\partial A,\partial B)
, the measure
\rho^{A,B}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}
is a product measure. It suffices to show that
\tilde{\rho}^{A,B}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}
is a product measure, and for that, we can assume that
F
is bounded. We write

\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2},N}^{A,B}[F]=C_{1}+C_{2},

where

C_{1}:=\frac{1}{|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})\in S_{1}\end{subarray}}\Big(\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}\tilde{\rho}_{z,N}^{\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}}(z)\sqcup\widetilde{A_{z}},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}}(x)\sqcup\widetilde{B_{z}}}\Big)\left[F\right]

for
S_{1}:=\{(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})\in W^{\textup{ext}_{N}}\,:\,\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}})=\mathbf{n}_{1},\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})=\mathbf{n}_{2},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}}\cap\widetilde{A}=\emptyset,\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}}\cap\widetilde{B}=\emptyset,(\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}}\sqcup\tilde{A})\cap(\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}}\sqcup\tilde{B})=\emptyset\}
,
and

C_{2}:=\frac{1}{|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})\in S_{2}\end{subarray}}\Big(\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}\tilde{\rho}_{z,N}^{\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}}(z)\sqcup\widetilde{A_{z}},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}}(x)\sqcup\widetilde{B_{z}}}\Big)\left[F\right]

for
S_{2}:=\{(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})\in W^{\textup{ext}_{N}}\,:\,\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}})=\mathbf{n}_{1},\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})=\mathbf{n}_{2},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}}\cap\widetilde{A}=\emptyset,\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}}\cap\widetilde{B}=\emptyset,(\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}}\sqcup\tilde{A})\cap(\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}}\sqcup\tilde{B})\neq\emptyset\}
.
Notice that because of the symmetries of the model, all the product measures appearing in
C_{1}
are equal. Thus, for a fixed pair of currents
(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}},\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}})
satisfying the conditions of the sum of the numerator in
C_{1}
, one finds,

C_{1}=\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}\left(\tilde{\rho}_{z,N}^{\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\textup{ext}}(z)\sqcup\widetilde{A_{z}},\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\textup{ext}}(x)\sqcup\widetilde{B_{z}}}\right)\left[F\right]\frac{1}{|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)|}|S_{1}|.

Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
3.42
we obtain

|S_{1}|\geq\prod_{i=1}^{2}\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\binom{N_{x,y}}{\mathbf{n}_{i}(x,y)}^{2}\mathbf{n}_{i}(x,y)!\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\binom{N_{x,\mathfrak{g}}}{\mathbf{n}_{i}(x,\mathfrak{g})},

where
N_{x,y}=N-\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)-\Delta\mathbf{n}(y)-A_{x}-A_{y}-B_{x}-B_{y}
for
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}
, hence

\frac{1}{|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)|}|S_{1}|\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}1,

(3.10)

which implies that along
(N_{k})_{k\geq 1}
the term
C_{1}
converges to a product measure. To handle
C_{2}
, notice that

|C_{2}|\leq\left(\prod_{z\in\Lambda}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\leq\Delta\mathbf{n}_{1}(z)+A_{z}\\
p\leq\Delta\mathbf{n}_{2}(z)+B_{z}\end{subarray}}\langle\varphi_{z}^{k}\rangle_{0}\langle\varphi_{z}^{p}\rangle_{0}+o(1)\right)\|F\|_{\infty}\frac{1}{|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)|}|S_{2}|.

It follows from (
3.10
) that

\frac{1}{|W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2};A,B)|}|S_{2}|=o(1),

hence
C_{2}
tends to
0
as
N
tends to infinity.
∎

3.5.3
Proof of Proposition
3.28

We now turn to the proof of Proposition
3.28
. As explained above, we will argue that the main contribution in the weights
\overline{w}_{N,A,\beta}(\mathbf{n})
comes from currents
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}
which belong to the set
W_{N}
introduced in Definition
3.27
. We start by showing that this contribution admits an explicit limit, which is this first part of Proposition
3.28
.

Lemma 3.45
.

For all
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
and
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
,

\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W_{N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}w_{\beta}^{A}(\mathbf{n}).

Proof.

Fix
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}})
and
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
. Notice that for every
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W_{N}
, we have a unique decomposition
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}
with
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in W^{\textup{int}}_{N}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}
(these sets were introduced in Definition
3.32
). This yields the factorization

w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}).

Now write,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W_{N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W_{N}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\cap\tilde{A}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in W^{\textup{int}}_{N}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\tilde{A}\sqcup\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}).

Notice that for
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W_{N}^{\textup{ext}}
satisfying
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}
, and
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\cap\tilde{A}=\emptyset
,

\dfrac{1}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\in W^{\textup{int}}_{N}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\tilde{A}\sqcup\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{1})=\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\mu^{0}_{x,N}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{A_{x}+\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\sigma_{(x,j)}\right].

Using this we get,

\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W_{N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=(c_{N}N)^{|A|}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\mu^{0}_{x,N}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{A_{x}+\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\sigma_{(x,j)}\right]\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\cap\tilde{A}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}).

(3.11)

Now, recall that
W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}\subset W_{2,N}
so that for
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}
with
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n}
, one has

w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}(\beta c_{N}^{2}J_{x,y})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}(\beta c_{N}h_{x})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,g}}.

This implies for the left hand side of (
3.11
) that

\text{(LHS)}=(c_{N}N)^{|A|}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\mu^{0}_{x,N}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{A_{x}+\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\sigma_{(x,j)}\right]\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}(\beta c_{N}^{2}J_{x,y})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}}\\
\times\prod_{x\in\Lambda}(\beta c_{N}h_{x})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,g}}|\{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}:\>\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n},\>\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\cap\tilde{A}=\emptyset\}|.

By (
3.8
),

|\{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\in W^{\textup{ext}}_{N}:\>\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2})=\mathbf{n},\>\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{2}\cap\tilde{A}=\emptyset\}|=(1+o(1))\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\frac{N^{2\mathbf{n}_{x,y}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\frac{N^{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}!},

(3.12)

hence

\text{(LHS)}=(1+o(1))\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\frac{(\beta J_{x,y})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\frac{(\beta h_{x})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}!}\\
\times\prod_{x\in\Lambda}(c_{N}N)^{A_{x}+\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\mu^{0}_{x,N}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{A_{x}+\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\sigma_{(x,j)}\right].

Using Lemma
3.26
, we obtain that

\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in W_{N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\prod_{\{x,y\}\subset\Lambda}\frac{(\beta J_{x,y})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,y}!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\frac{(\beta h_{x})^{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}}}{\mathbf{n}_{x,\mathfrak{g}}!}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle\varphi^{A_{x}+\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\right\rangle_{0}

which is the desired result.
∎

We now prove the second part of Proposition
3.28
by induction on
|\mathbf{n}|
, namely (
3.5
). We only write the proof in the special case
h=0
in which the ghost
\mathfrak{g}
plays no role. It is easy to extend the result to the general case
h\geq 0
(see Remark
3.46
below).

Proof of second half of Proposition
3.28
.

Recall that
h=0
. We proceed by induction on
|\mathbf{n}|
. For
k\geq 0
, introduce
\mathbf{H}_{k}
: For every
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}
with
|\mathbf{n}|=k
, and every
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
,

\overline{w}_{N,A,\beta}(\mathbf{n})\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}w_{\beta}^{A}(\mathbf{n}).

First, if
|\mathbf{n}|=0
, then, proceeding as in the proof of Lemma
3.45
, we get

\overline{w}_{N,A,\beta}(\mathbf{n})=\prod_{x\in\Lambda}(c_{N}N)^{A_{x}+\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\mu_{x,N}^{0}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{A_{x}+\Delta\mathbf{n}(x)}\sigma_{(x,j)}\right],

(3.13)

and the right-hand side in (
3.13
) converges to
w_{A,\beta}(\mathbf{n})
by Lemma
3.26
.

Let
k\geq 0
and assume that
\mathbf{H}_{i}
are true for all
0\leq i\leq k
. Let
\mathbf{n}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}
such that
|\mathbf{n}|=k+1
. By Lemma
3.45
, it is sufficient to prove that
\alpha_{1}(N)
and
\alpha_{2}(N)
vanish where

\alpha_{i}(N):=\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda}\setminus W_{i,N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}).

We first prove that
\alpha_{1}(N)\rightarrow 0
. Notice that

\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda}\setminus W_{1,N}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in\Lambda\\
u\neq v\end{subarray}}\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq A_{u}}\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq N}V_{i,j}(u,v),

where

V_{i,j}(u,v):=\{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda},\>\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(u,i,v,j)\geq 1\}.

Then, by substracting
1
to the weight of the edge
\{(u,i),(v,j)\}
for every
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in V_{i,j}(u,v)
, we get

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(N)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{u\neq v}\sum_{i=1}^{A_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in V_{i,j}(u,v)\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{u\neq v}\sum_{i=1}^{A_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\beta J_{u,v}c_{N}^{2}\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{m}}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{m}})=\mathbf{n}^{-}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{m}}=\tilde{A}_{i,j}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{m}})

where
\mathbf{n}^{-}
is the current satisfying
\mathbf{n}^{-}_{e}=\mathbf{n}_{e}
for all edges
e\neq uv
, and
\mathbf{n}^{-}_{uv}=\mathbf{n}_{uv}-1
; and
\tilde{A}_{i,j}=\tilde{A}\Delta\{(u,i),(v,j)\}
. Notice that
\tilde{A}_{i,j}=\tilde{A}\setminus\{(u,i),(v,j)\}
if
1\leq j\leq A_{v}
, and
\tilde{A}_{i,j}=\left(\tilde{A}\setminus\{(u,i)\}\right)\cup\{(v,j)\}
otherwise. With this in mind, we associate to these new sets of sources their corresponding elements in
\mathcal{M}
: define
A^{\prime}
from
A
by letting
A^{\prime}_{u}=A_{u}-1
,
A^{\prime}_{v}=A_{v}-1
, and
A^{\prime}_{z}=A_{z}
otherwise; also define
A^{\prime\prime}
from
A
by letting
A_{u}^{\prime\prime}=A_{u}-1
,
A_{v}^{\prime\prime}=A_{v}+1
, and
A_{z}^{\prime\prime}=A_{z}
otherwise. Then, using the induction hypothesis (
|\mathbf{n}^{-}|=|\mathbf{n}|-1
),

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(N)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\neq v\\
1\leq i\leq A_{u}\end{subarray}}\beta J_{u,v}c_{N}^{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{A_{v}}(c_{N}N)^{2}\overline{w}_{N,A^{\prime},\beta}(\mathbf{n}^{-})+\sum_{j=A_{v}+1}^{N}\overline{w}_{N,A^{\prime\prime},\beta}(\mathbf{n}^{-})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\neq v\\
1\leq i\leq A_{u}\end{subarray}}\beta J_{u,v}c_{N}^{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{A_{v}}(c_{N}N)^{2}O(1)+\sum_{j=A_{v}+1}^{N}O(1)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(1/\sqrt{N})\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

We used the fact that
(c_{N}N)^{2}c_{N}^{2}\rightarrow 0
and
c_{N}^{2}N\rightarrow 0
. Moreover, to bound the second sum in the right-hand side of the first inequality, we used the symmetries of the model to write that for all
A_{v}+1\leq j\leq N
,

\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{m}}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{m}})=\mathbf{n}^{-}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{m}}=\tilde{A}_{i,j}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{m}})=\overline{w}_{N,A^{\prime\prime},\beta}(\mathbf{n}^{-}).

We now prove that
\alpha_{2}(N)\rightarrow 0.
The idea is essentially the same: we seek a non-zero weight between to different blocks that we diminish to apply the induction hypothesis. Notice that if
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{g}}}\setminus W_{2,N}
then there exist
u\in\Lambda
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, such that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(u,i)\in e\\
e\not\subset B_{u,N}\end{subarray}}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{e}\geq 2.

There are two cases: either for some
(v,j)
with
u\neq v
, one has
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(u,i,v,j)\geq 2
and in such case we can subtract
2
to the weight of this edge (which does not change the set of sources) to gain a
(c_{N}^{2})^{2}
which is in competition with a factor
O(N^{2})
, and hence vanishes; or we can find two distinct vertices
(v,j)
and
(w,k)
with
u\neq v,w
such that
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(u,i,v,j)=1
and
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(u,i,w,k)=1
so that the deletion of these weights adds two new sources,
(v,j)
and
(w,k)
, and gives a factor
(c_{N}^{2})^{2}
which is in competition with the factor
(c_{N}N)^{-2}O(N^{3})=O(N^{5/2})
, and once again vanishes. More formally, define
X_{1,N}
(resp.
X_{2,N}
) to be the subset of currents of
\Omega^{N}_{\Lambda_{g}}\setminus W_{2,N}
that falls into the first case (resp. second case). Then,

\displaystyle\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in X_{1,N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{u\neq v}\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\sum_{1\leq j\leq N}\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(u,i,v,j)\geq 2\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{u\neq v}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq N}(\beta J_{u,v}c_{N}^{2})^{2}\overline{w}_{N,A,\beta}(\mathbf{n}^{2-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(N^{2}c_{N}^{4})\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0,

where
\mathbf{n}^{2-}
is the current satisfying
\mathbf{n}^{2-}_{e}=\mathbf{n}_{e}
for all edges
e\neq uv
and
\mathbf{n}^{2-}_{u,v}=\mathbf{n}_{u,v}-2
. We have used the induction hypothesis on the last line (since
|\mathbf{n}^{2-}|=|\mathbf{n}|-2
).

Now, for the second case, bear in mind that if we can find, for
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in X_{2,N}
,
(u,i),(v,j)
and
(w,k)
satisfying the properties described above, with one of them belonging to
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}
then
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}
belongs to
\Omega_{\Lambda}^{N}\setminus W_{1,N}
and the contribution of such currents goes to zero since
\alpha_{1}(N)\rightarrow 0
. Thus,

\displaystyle\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in X_{2,N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\leq\alpha_{1}(N)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v,w\in\Lambda\\
v\neq u\\
w\neq u\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=A_{u}+1}^{N}\sum_{j=A_{v}+1}^{N}\sum_{k=A_{w}+1}^{N}\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(u,i,v,j)=1\\
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(u,i,w,k)=1\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}}).

Notice that for
i\geq A_{u}+1
,
j\geq A_{v}+1
and
k\geq A_{w}+1
,

\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(u,i,v,j)=1\\
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}(u,i,w,k)=1\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\beta^{2}J_{u,v}J_{u,w}c_{N}^{4}(c_{N}N)^{-2}\overline{w}_{N,B,\beta}(\mathbf{n}^{-,-})

where
B\in\mathcal{M}
is defined from
A
by only changing its values on
v
and
w
where
B_{v}=A_{v}+1
and
B_{w}=A_{w}+1
, and
\mathbf{n}^{-,-}
is obtained from
\mathbf{n}
by reducing by
1
the weights of the edges
uv
and
uw
. We can once again apply the induction hypothesis to get

\dfrac{(c_{N}N)^{|A|}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|\Lambda|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in X_{2,N}\\
\Theta_{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})=\mathbf{n}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\tilde{A}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\leq O(c_{N}^{2}N)\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

We finally obtained
\alpha_{2}(N)\rightarrow 0
which yields
\mathbf{H}_{k+1}
.
∎

Remark 3.46
.

In the case where we add an external field, the preceding proof may be adapted using the following ideas. For the vanishing of
\alpha_{1}(N)
above, adding the external field means considering the case where
v=\mathfrak{g}
which adds a
O(c_{N}(c_{N}N))
term (because in this case removing
1
to the weight of the edge reduces by
1
the number of sources in
\Lambda_{N}
). For
\alpha_{2}(N)
, in the first case, consider the situation where
v
is equal to
\mathfrak{g}
which turns a
O(N^{2}c_{N}^{4})
into a
O(Nc_{N}^{2})
; and in the second case, consider the situation where
v
or
w
is equal to
\mathfrak{g}
which turns a
O((c_{N}^{2})^{2}N^{5/2})
into a
O((c_{N}^{3})(c_{N}N)^{-1}N^{2})=O(c_{N}^{2}N)
(because in this case we add exactly one source in
\Lambda_{N}
).

4
Positive probability of admissible partitions

The goal of this section is to prove that any element of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}^{A,B}(z)
has positive probability under
\rho^{A,B}_{z,\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}
. In particular, this shows that the distribution of tanglings in each block is not deterministic. This of crucial importance for the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Let
\Lambda
