\displaystyle\sqrt{\langle\varphi_{x^{\prime}}^{2}\rangle_{B_{n}}^{\mathfrak{p}}\langle\varphi_{y^{\prime}}^{2}\rangle_{B_{n}}^{\mathfrak{p}}}\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{B_{n}}^{\mathfrak{p}}\mathbf{P}^{xy}_{B_{N}\sqcup\{\mathfrak{g}\}}[x\longleftrightarrow y\text{ in }B_{m}].

This is sufficient to conclude since for
n\geq 1
sufficiently large one has
\langle\varphi_{x^{\prime}}^{2}\rangle_{B_{n}}^{\mathfrak{p}},\langle\varphi_{y^{\prime}}^{2}\rangle_{B_{n}}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq 2\langle\varphi_{x}^{2}\rangle^{+}<\infty
.

We now prove (
6.12
). To do this, we analyse the prelimit. Defining
h(\mathfrak{p})_{z}:=\sum_{y\notin B_{n}}J_{z,y}\mathfrak{p}_{y}
for all
z\in B_{n}
, we have that

\mu_{B_{n},N,\beta}^{h(\mathfrak{p})}\left[\Phi_{x^{\prime},N}\Phi_{y^{\prime},N}\right]
tends to

\langle\varphi_{x^{\prime}}\varphi_{y^{\prime}}\rangle_{B_{n}}^{\mathfrak{p}}
as
N\to\infty
,
where
\mu_{B_{n},N,\beta}^{h(\mathfrak{p})}
has been defined in Definition
3.23
, and where we have used Proposition
3.24
. Note that by the symmetries of the block-spins for any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,

\mu_{B_{n},N,\beta}^{h(\mathfrak{p})}[\sigma_{\tilde{x}^{\prime}}\sigma_{\tilde{y}^{\prime}}]=\mu_{B_{n},N,\beta}^{h(\mathfrak{p})}[\sigma_{(x^{\prime},i)}\sigma_{(y^{\prime},j)}],

where
\tilde{x}^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},1)
and
\tilde{y}^{\prime}=(y^{\prime},1)
, so that the above convergence can be rephrased as

(c_{N}N)^{2}\mu_{B_{n},N,\beta}^{h(\mathfrak{p})}\left[\sigma_{\tilde{x}^{\prime}}\sigma_{\tilde{y}^{\prime}}\right]\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\langle\varphi_{x^{\prime}}\varphi_{y^{\prime}}\rangle_{B_{n}}^{\mathfrak{p}}.

(6.13)

Recall the definition of the Hamiltonian of the Ising measure
\mu_{B_{n},N,\beta}^{h(\mathfrak{p})}
on
B_{n}\times K_{N}
from Section
3.5.1
and note that

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{h(\mathfrak{p})}_{B_{n},\beta,N}(\sigma)=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in B_{n}\\
j,k\in\{1,\cdots,N\}\end{subarray}}J_{N}((u,i),(v,j))\sigma_{(u,i)}\sigma_{(v,j)}-\beta c_{N}\sum_{(u,j)\in B_{n}\times K_{N}}h(\mathfrak{p})_{u}\sigma_{(u,j)},

where
J_{N}
is defined implicitly. Hence, we may as well write
\mu_{B_{n},N,\beta,J_{N}}^{h(\mathfrak{p})}=\mu_{B_{n},N,\beta}^{h(\mathfrak{p})}
. This notation allows us later to consider interactions other than
J_{N}
.

By Proposition
3.28
, the left-hand side of (
6.12
) can be obtained as the limit of

\frac{(c_{N}N)^{4}}{\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|B_{n}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{B_{n}\sqcup\{\mathfrak{g}\}}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\{\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime}\}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{B}_{m}^{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})\}

as
N
goes to infinity, where
\tilde{z}=(z,1)
for
z\in\{x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\}
and

\mathcal{B}_{m}^{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime}):=\{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{B_{n}\cup\{\mathfrak{g}\}}:\>\tilde{x}\overset{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\tilde{y}\text{ in }B_{m}\times K_{N},\>\tilde{x}\overset{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}}\tilde{x}^{\prime},\>\tilde{y}\overset{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}}\tilde{y}^{\prime}\}.

Now, denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{x}}
the cluster of
\tilde{x}
. We say that a subset of
B_{n}\times K_{N}\cup\{\mathfrak{g}\}
is admissible if it satisfies
\{\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{x}}=C\}\subset\mathcal{B}_{m}^{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})
. If
C
is admissible, then

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{B_{n}\sqcup\{\mathfrak{g}\}}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\{\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime}\}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta,h(\mathfrak{p})}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{x}}=C\}=\mu^{h^{C}(\mathfrak{p})}_{B_{n},N,\beta,J_{N}^{C}}[\sigma_{\tilde{x}^{\prime}}\sigma_{\tilde{y}^{\prime}}]\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{B_{n}\sqcup\{\mathfrak{g}\}}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}\}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta,h(\mathfrak{p})}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{x}}=C\},

where
J_{N}^{C}
is defined by

J_{N}^{C}((u,i),(v,j))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}J_{N}((u,i),(v,j))&\mbox{if }(u,i),(v,j)\notin C\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

and
h^{C}(\mathfrak{p})=0
if
\mathfrak{g}\in C
and
h^{C}(\mathfrak{p})=h(\mathfrak{p})
otherwise.
Since
J_{N}^{C}\leq J_{N}
and
h^{C}(\mathfrak{p})\leq h(\mathfrak{p})
, by Griffiths’ inequality,

\mu^{h^{C}(\mathfrak{p})}_{B_{n},N,\beta,J_{N}^{C}}[\sigma_{\tilde{x}^{\prime}}\sigma_{\tilde{y}^{\prime}}]\leq\mu^{h(\mathfrak{p})}_{B_{n},N,\beta,J_{N}}[\sigma_{\tilde{x}^{\prime}}\sigma_{\tilde{y}^{\prime}}],

so that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{B_{n}\sqcup\{\mathfrak{g}\}}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\{\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime}\}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{x}}=C\}\leq\mu^{h(\mathfrak{p})}_{B_{n},N,\beta,J_{N}}[\sigma_{\tilde{x}^{\prime}}\sigma_{\tilde{y}^{\prime}}]\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{B_{n}\sqcup\{\mathfrak{g}\}}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}\}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{x}}=C\}.

Summing over
C
admissible yields

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{B_{n}\sqcup\{\mathfrak{g}\}}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\{\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime}\}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{B}_{m}^{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})\}\leq\mu^{h(\mathfrak{p})}_{B_{n},N,\beta,J_{N}}[\sigma_{\tilde{x}^{\prime}}\sigma_{\tilde{y}^{\prime}}]

(6.14)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\Omega^{N}_{B_{n}\sqcup\{\mathfrak{g}\}}\\
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}\}\end{subarray}}w_{N,\beta}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{B}_{m}^{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})\}.

Multiplying by
(c_{N}N)^{4}/\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{|B_{n}|}
on each side of (
6.14
) and letting
N
go to infinity yields (
6.12
) by Proposition
3.28
and (
6.13
).
∎

We now finish the proof of Theorem
1.2
. It remains to prove that (
1.10
) is sufficient to establish that
|\mathcal{G}(\beta)|=1
. By Proposition
C.2
it suffices to show that
m^{*}(\beta)=0
. From the preceding arguments, we know that
\langle\cdot\rangle^{0}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}
coincide on even functions, so condition (
1.10
) reads

\liminf_{d_{G}(o,x)\rightarrow\infty}\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle^{0}_{\beta}=\liminf_{d_{G}(o,x)\rightarrow\infty}\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=0.

However, by Griffiths’ inequality and
\Gamma
-invariance, for all
x\in V
,

\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}\geq m^{*}(\beta)^{2}

which implies that
m^{*}(\beta)=0
.
\hfill\square

7
Continuity of the phase transition

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.3
. Fix a reflection positive interaction
J
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
in the sense of
[
Bis09
, Definition 5.1 and Lemma 5.5]
(see also
[
Pan23
, Section 3]
for a complete introduction to the subject in the case of the
\varphi^{4}
model). We suppress
J
from the notation when clear from context. For each such
J
, recall the definition of the associated random walk
(X_{k})_{k\geq 0}
(started at
0
) with step distribution given by

\mathbb{P}[X_{n+1}=y\>|\>X_{n}=x]=\frac{J_{x,y}}{|J|}

where
|J|:=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}J_{0,x}
. It is known that
(X_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is transient if and only if

\int_{[-\pi,\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{1-\mathbb{E}[e^{ip\cdot X_{1}}]}\frac{\textup{d}p}{(2\pi)^{d}}<\infty.

(7.1)

A proof of this statement can be found in
[
LL10
, Proposition 4.2.3]
; it crucially uses the reflection symmetry and aperiodicity of the random walk.

We now state a version of the infrared bound
[
FSS76
]
that is suited to our setting.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
J
be reflection positive and such that the associated random walk is transient, i.e. (
7.1
) holds. For any
(v_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
of finite support, and any
\beta<\beta_{c}(J)
,

\sum_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}v_{x}\overline{v_{y}}\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta}^{0}\leq\frac{1}{2\beta|J|}\sum_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}v_{x}\overline{v_{y}}G(x,y)

(7.2)

where
G
is the Green’s function of
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
given by

G(x,y):=\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{L^{d}}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{T}_{L}^{*}}\frac{e^{ip\cdot(x-y)}}{1-\mathbb{E}[e^{ip\cdot X_{1}}]}=\int_{[-\pi,\pi)^{d}}\frac{e^{ip\cdot(x-y)}}{1-\mathbb{E}[e^{ip\cdot X_{1}}]}\frac{\textup{d}p}{(2\pi)^{d}}<\infty.

Proof.

The convergence and finiteness of
G(x,y)
is a consequence of (
7.1
). The infrared bound (
7.2
) is a direct consequence of
[
Bis09
, Lemma 4.5]
together with the fact that the spontaneous magnetisation vanishes for
\beta<\beta_{c}
. We stress a subtle point in the reasoning above: one can show, by using similar regularity estimates as in Corollary
D.12
and arguing as in the proof of
[
LP76
, Theorem 4.5]
, that the
\varphi^{4}
measures on
\mathbb{T}_{L}^{d}
converge weakly as
L\rightarrow\infty
to the unique (since
\beta<\beta_{c}
, see Proposition
C.2
) Gibbs measure on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
∎

We now turn to the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

By left-continuity of
\beta\mapsto\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta}^{0}
for
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we can also extend (
7.2
) to
\beta=\beta_{c}
. Using equation (
7.2
) with
v
defined by
v_{x}=|B_{n}|^{-2}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{n}}(x)
yields

\frac{1}{|B_{n}|^{2}}\sum_{x,y\in B_{n}}\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle_{\beta_{c}}^{0}\leq\frac{1}{2\beta_{c}|J|}\frac{1}{|B_{n}|^{2}}\sum_{x,y\in B_{n}}G(x,y).

(7.3)

The Riemann–Lebesgue Lemma gives that
G(x,y)\underset{|x-y|_{1}\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
. In particular,

\frac{1}{|B_{n}|^{2}}\sum_{x,y\in B_{n}}G(x,y)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

By positivity of correlations we have that the lefthand side of (
7.3
) converges to
0
, which in turn implies that (
1.10
) holds at
\beta=\beta_{c}
. By Theorem
1.2
, one concludes that
|\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}(\beta_{c})|=1
, and therefore
m^{*}(\beta_{c})=0
by Proposition
C.2
.
∎

Appendix A
Right-continuity of the spontaneous magnetisation

Proposition A.1
.

The spontaneous magnetisation
m^{*}(\beta)
is right-continuous at any
\beta>0
.

Proof.

Let
\beta>0
. Then,for any
\Lambda\subset V
with
\Lambda\ni o
finite we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{\delta\to 0^{+}}m^{*}(\beta+\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta+\delta}+\varepsilon(\Lambda,\beta)=\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}+\varepsilon(\Lambda,\beta),

where
\varepsilon(\Lambda,\beta)=\sup_{\delta\in[0,1]}\langle\varphi_{o}\mathbbm{1}_{\exists x\notin\Lambda:\>|\varphi_{x}|>\mathfrak{p}_{x}(M)}\rangle^{+}_{\beta+\delta}
. Here we used the monotonicity in boundary conditions (see Proposition
B.4
) and that
\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta+\delta}
is a continuous function of
\delta
. Since
\varepsilon(\beta,\Lambda)
tends to
0
as
\Lambda
increases to
V
by the continuity of the regularity constants— see Proposition
2.2
— we have

\limsup_{\delta\to 0^{+}}m^{*}(\beta+\delta)\leq m^{*}(\beta).

On the other hand, we have that
m^{*}(\beta+\delta)\geq m^{*}(\beta)
for any
\delta>0
, hence

\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}m^{*}(\beta+\delta)=m^{*}(\beta),

as desired.
∎

Appendix B
Correlation inequalities

In this section, all the boundary conditions considered are implicitly assumed to satisfy the summability condition defined in the introduction, i.e.
\sum_{y\notin\Lambda}J_{x,y}\eta_{y}<\infty~~\forall x\in\Lambda
.

B.1
The FKG and absolute value FKG inequalities

Proposition B.1

(FKG inequality,
[
GJ12
, Theorem 4.4.1]
)
.

Let
\Lambda\subset V
finite,
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
. Then, for any increasing and bounded functions
F,G:\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\langle F(\varphi)G(\varphi)\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}^{\eta}\geq\langle F(\varphi)\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}^{\eta}\langle G(\varphi)\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,h}^{\eta}.

Proposition B.2

(Absolute value FKG)
.

Let
\Lambda\subset V
finite,
\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{V}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\Lambda}
. Then, for any bounded increasing functions
F,G:(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\Lambda}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\langle F(|\varphi|)G(|\varphi|)\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}\geq\langle F(|\varphi|)\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}\langle G(|\varphi|)\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}.

Proof.

We rewrite the Hamiltonian as

H^{\eta}_{\Lambda}(\varphi):=\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda}V_{x,y}(\varphi_{x}-\varphi_{y})-\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda}J_{x,y}\frac{\varphi_{x}^{2}+\varphi_{y}^{2}}{2}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Lambda\\
y\in V\setminus\Lambda\end{subarray}}J_{x,y}\varphi_{x}\eta_{y}-\sum_{x\in\Lambda}h_{x}\varphi_{x},

where
V_{x,y}(z)=\frac{1}{2}J_{x,y}z^{2}
. Note that
V_{x,y}
is convex, symmetric, i.e.
V_{x,y}(-z)=V_{x,y}(z)
, and the second derivative of
V_{x,y}
is a non-increasing function.
The desired assertion follows from
[
LO24
, Corollary 6.4]
.
∎

B.2
Proof of the Ginibre inequality

Recall that if
A:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is finitely supported, we write

\varphi_{A}=\prod_{x\in V}\varphi_{x}^{A_{x}}.

Let
A,B:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be finitely supported. Let
\Lambda
be a finite subset of
V
that contains the support of
A
and
B
, and let
|\eta|\leq\eta^{\prime}
outside
\Lambda
. We want to prove that

\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\eta^{\prime}}_{\Lambda,\beta}-\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}\geq\left|\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle^{\eta^{\prime}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}-\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\eta}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\eta^{\prime}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\right|.

Let
h_{x}:=\sum_{y\notin\Lambda}J_{x,y}\eta_{y}
and
h_{x}^{\prime}:=\sum_{y\notin\Lambda}J_{x,y}\eta_{y}^{\prime}
. Then, with the notation of Section
3.5.1
, using the Ginibre inequality of the Ising model on
\Lambda_{N}=\Lambda\times K_{N}
, and linearity,

\Big|\langle\Phi_{A,N}\rangle^{0}_{\Lambda_{N},\beta,h^{\prime}}\langle\Phi_{B,N}\rangle^{0}_{\Lambda_{N},\beta,h}-\langle\Phi_{A,N}\rangle^{0}_{\Lambda_{N},\beta,h}\langle\Phi_{B,N}\rangle^{0}_{\Lambda_{N},\beta,h^{\prime}}\Big|\\
\leq\langle\Phi_{A,N}\Phi_{B,N}\rangle^{0}_{\Lambda_{N},\beta,h^{\prime}}-\langle\Phi_{A,N}\Phi_{B,N}\rangle^{0}_{\Lambda_{N},\beta,h}

where

\Phi_{A,N}:=\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\Phi_{x,N}^{A_{x}},\Phi_{B,N}:=\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\Phi_{x,N}^{B_{x}}.

Now, taking the limit as
N
goes to infinity and using Proposition
3.24
, we get the desired result. Note that we used the interpretation of boundary conditions as non-homogeneous external fields. Moreover, if
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}\in\mathcal{G}(\beta)
,

\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}-\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\beta}\geq\left|\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle_{\beta}-\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle_{\beta}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}\right|.

(B.1)

Indeed, if we fix
\eta^{\prime}=\mathfrak{p}
above and integrate against
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}
among
\eta
satisfying
|\eta|\leq\mathfrak{p}
outside
\Lambda
,

\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{\forall x\notin\Lambda,\>|\eta_{x}|\leq\mathfrak{p}_{x}}\rangle_{\beta}-\Big\langle\langle\varphi_{A}\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}\mathbbm{1}_{\forall x\notin\Lambda,\>|\eta_{x}|\leq\mathfrak{p}_{x}}\Big\rangle_{\beta}\\
\geq\Big|\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\Big\langle\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}\mathbbm{1}_{\forall x\notin\Lambda,\>|\eta_{x}|\leq\mathfrak{p}_{x}}\Big\rangle_{\beta}-\Big\langle\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}\mathbbm{1}_{\forall x\notin\Lambda,\>|\eta_{x}|\leq\mathfrak{p}_{x}}\Big\rangle_{\beta}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Lambda,\beta}\Big|.

Using the results of Section
2
and taking
\Lambda\uparrow V
yields (
B.1
).

\square

B.3
Monotonicity in boundary conditions

We have the following finite volume monotonicity properties for correlations and increasing functions. They follow from the Ginibre inequality and the FKG inequality respectively.

Proposition B.3
.

Let
\Lambda\subset V
finite. Let
A:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be a degree function. For
|\eta|\leq\eta^{\prime}
,

\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}\leq\langle\varphi_{A}\rangle^{\eta^{\prime}}_{\Lambda,\beta}.

Proposition B.4
.

Let
\eta\leq\eta^{\prime}
. Then, for any
f
increasing and integrable,

\langle f\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta}\leq\langle f\rangle^{\eta^{\prime}}_{\Lambda,\beta}.

B.4
Single-site moment bounds

Proposition B.5
.

There exists
C>0
such that for all
k\geq 0
,

\langle\varphi^{2k}\rangle_{0}\leq C\langle\varphi^{2k+2}\rangle_{0}.

Proof.

Note that there exists
C>0
such that for every
k\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{\langle\varphi^{2k}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\varphi^{2k+2}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{\int_{-1}^{1}s^{2k}\rho(\text{d}s)}{\int_{-1}^{1}s^{2k+2}\rho(\text{ds})}+1\leq\dfrac{M}{m}\dfrac{2k+3}{2k+1}+1\leq C,

where

M:=\max_{s\in[-1,1]}e^{-gs^{4}-as^{2}}\text{ and }m:=\min_{s\in[-1,1]}e^{-gs^{4}-as^{2}}.

∎

Appendix C
Uniqueness of the phase transition

Define,

\displaystyle\beta_{c}^{\textup{MAG}}

\displaystyle:=\inf\Big\{\beta>0:\text{ }\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}>0\Big\},

(C.1)

\displaystyle\beta_{c}^{\textup{LRO}}

\displaystyle:=\inf\Big\{\beta>0:\text{ }\inf_{x\in V}\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle^{0}_{\beta}>0\Big\},

(C.2)

\displaystyle\beta_{c}^{\textup{SUSC}}

\displaystyle:=\inf\Big\{\beta>0:\>\sum_{x\in V}\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle_{\beta}^{0}=\infty\Big\}.

(C.3)

Proposition C.1
.

Let
G
be a countably infinite and vertex-transitive graph of polynomial growth. Then,

\beta_{c}^{\textup{MAG}}=\beta_{c}^{\textup{LRO}}=\beta_{c}^{\textup{SUSC}}.

Proof.

The assertion that
\beta_{c}^{\textup{MAG}}=\beta_{c}^{\textup{SUSC}}
was established in
[
ABF87
]
. We now prove that
\beta_{c}^{\textup{MAG}}=\beta_{c}^{\textup{LRO}}
. Let
\beta>\beta_{c}^{\textup{LRO}}
and write
c:=\inf_{x\in V}\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle^{0}_{\beta}>0
. Then, by monotonicity in the boundary conditions and the mixing of
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
we have

c\leq\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle^{0}_{\beta}\leq\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}\underset{d_{G}(o,x)\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\big[\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}\big]^{2}.

This implies that
\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}>0
. Let now
\beta<\beta_{c}^{\textup{LRO}}
. Consider some
\beta<\beta^{\prime}<\beta_{c}^{\textup{LRO}}
. It follows from (
2.4
) that

\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}\leq\langle\varphi_{o}\varphi_{x}\rangle^{0}_{\beta^{\prime}}.

Letting
d_{G}(o,x)\rightarrow\infty
and using the mixing of
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
as above we obtain that

\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=0
. The desired assertion follows.
∎

Proposition C.2
.

Let
G
be as in the proposition above. Let
\beta>0
. The following are equivalent,

(i)

|\mathcal{G}(\beta)|=1
,

(ii)

\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=\langle\cdot\rangle^{-}_{\beta}
,

(iii)

\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{-}_{\beta}=0
.

Proof.

The implication
(i)\implies(ii)
is trivial. For the implication
(ii)\implies(iii)
, note that if
\varphi\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
, then
-\varphi\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{-}_{\beta}
. Hence,
\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=-\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{-}_{\beta}
, which implies that
\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle^{-}_{\beta}=0
.

Finally, to show that
(iii)\implies(i)
, let
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}\in\mathcal{G}(\beta)
. Using Proposition
2.6
and Strassen’s theorem
[
Str65
]
we obtain a coupling
(\mathbb{P},\varphi^{+},\varphi)
,
\varphi^{+}\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
,
\varphi\sim\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}
, such that
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely,
\varphi^{+}_{x}\geq\varphi_{x}
for every
x\in V
. Again by Proposition
2.6
we have
\langle\varphi_{x}\rangle_{\beta}=\langle\varphi_{x}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}=0
for every
x\in V
. It follows that
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely,
\varphi^{+}_{x}=\varphi_{x}
for every
x\in V
, hence
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}=\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
.
∎

Appendix D
Probabilistic estimates for Gibbs measures

In this section, we develop the probabilistic estimates for Gibbs measures associated to the
\varphi^{4}
model on graphs of polynomial growth (see also
[
Pan24
, Chapter 11]
for a slightly more general setting). The results in this section follow from adaptations of the foundational papers on the subject
[
LP76
]
and
[
Rue70
]
, which establish the theory for more general measures on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
We optimise our estimates to the case of
\varphi^{4}
, which has stronger concentration properties than the class considered in
[
LP76
]
.

D.1
Setup and main results

We start by recalling some notations and definitions. We work on a graph
G=(V,E)
which is connected, vertex-transitive graph and has polynomial growth, defined as follows. For a distinguished origin
o\in V
, we write
B_{k}
to denote the ball of radius
k\in\mathbb{N}
centred at
o
with respect to the graph distance
d_{G}(\cdot,\cdot)
. We assume that there exists
d\geq 1
(called the dimension of
G
) and
c_{G},C_{G}>0
such that

c_{G}k^{d}\leq|B_{k}|\leq C_{G}k^{d},\qquad\forall k\geq 1.

(D.1)

Remark D.1
.

Usually a graph is said to have (at most) polynomial growth if there are constants
d\geq 1
and
C_{G}>0
such that the upper bound
|B_{k}|\leq C_{G}k^{d}
is satisfied for every
k\geq 1
. That one can always find
c_{G},C_{G}
and
d
such that (
D.1
) holds follows from Gromov’s theorem
[
Gro81
]
.

Remark D.2
.

It was proved in
[
Tes07
]
(see also
[
BD13
]
for a relevant result) that for every vertex-transitive graph of polynomial growth there are constants
C>0
and
\delta>0
such that

|B_{n}\setminus B_{n-1}|\leq Cn^{-\delta}|B_{n}|\leq Cn^{-\delta}

for every
n\geq 1
.
In fact, this was proved under the weaker assumption that the graph satisfies a (uniform) doubling property, i.e. without the assumption of vertex-transitivity, which is easy to check in our case.
To the best of our knowledge, it is not known whether one can take
\delta=1
for all vertex-transitive graphs of polynomial growth.

Remark D.3
.

The complications in generalising the results of
[
LP76
,
Rue70
]
to the context of vertex-transitive graphs of polynomial growth arise from the fact that it is not known whether one can take
\delta=1
in the remark above. The results of the aforementioned articles apply to a much more general class of unbounded spin systems, which includes in particular the case
g=0
for some
a
chosen appropriately, and generalising our results to the whole class of unbounded spin systems considered there without a good understanding of the underlying graph structure seems rather complicated. The fact that we have a quartic term in our single site measure, i.e. that
g>0
, plays an important role in our analysis.

We fix a set of interactions
J=(J_{x,y})_{x,y\in V}
such that
J_{x,y}\geq 0
for all
x,y\in V
and

J_{x,y}\leq\Psi(d_{G}(x,y)),\text{ for all $x\neq y\in V$},

where
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is defined as
\Psi(k)=C_{\Psi}k^{-d-\varepsilon}
for some constants
C_{\Psi}>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. In addition, we fix
(\beta,h,g,a)
. Recall that for
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
, the
\varphi^{4}
model with these parameters on
\Lambda\subset V
is the measure
\langle\cdot\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}
defined by (
1.4
). In this section, we only keep the dependence on
\Lambda
explicit and it is sometimes convenient to write
\nu_{\Lambda}^{\eta}
to denote the corresponding measure.

For a Borel probability measure
\lambda
on
\mathbb{R}^{V}
, we write
\nu_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}
to denote the corresponding measure with random boundary conditions sampled from
\lambda
. Let

\Xi(M)=\{\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{V}:\,\exists\Lambda_{\varphi}\subset V\text{ finite},~\varphi^{4}_{x}\leq M\log d_{G}(o,x)~~\forall x\in V\setminus\Lambda_{\varphi}\}.

We call
\lambda
a law on weakly growing boundary conditions if
\lambda
is a Borel probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{V}
supported on
\Xi(M)
for some
M>0
. Finally, for
\Delta\subset\Lambda
, we write
\nu_{\Delta}^{(\Lambda),\lambda}
to denote the restriction of
\nu_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}
to
\mathbb{R}^{\Delta}
, i.e. the measure defined by

\nu_{\Delta}^{(\Lambda),\lambda}(\varphi_{\Delta})=\frac{1}{Z_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}}e^{-U^{\lambda}_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{\Delta}\sqcup\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta},

where
\varphi_{\Delta}\sqcup\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}
denotes the concatenation of two configurations and

U^{\lambda}_{\Lambda}(\varphi)=\beta H^{\lambda}_{\Lambda}(\varphi)+\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\Big(g\varphi_{x}^{4}+a\varphi_{x}^{2}\Big)

is the potential. We now recall the definition of regular measures. Let
\nu
be a measure on
\mathbb{R}^{V}
. For
\gamma,\delta>0
, we say
\nu
is
(\gamma,\delta)
-regular if, for every
\Lambda\subset V
finite, the density of the projection measure satisfies

\frac{\textup{d}\nu}{\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda}}(\varphi_{\Lambda})\leq e^{-\sum_{x\in\Lambda}(\gamma\varphi_{x}^{4}-\delta)},

where
\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
. Note that, for any
\Lambda\subset V
finite and
\lambda
a law on weakly growing boundary conditions,
\nu_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}
is
(\gamma,\delta)
-regular with constants depending only on
(\beta,h,g,a)
. Moreover, the constants are continuous in these dependencies and uniform over all subsets of
\Lambda
. This is a direct consequence of repeatedly applying Hölder’s inequalities on
|H_{\Lambda}(\varphi)|
.

Finally, we restate the definition of a Gibbs measure. Recall that a Borel probability measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{V}
is
tempered
if:

•

for every
\Lambda\subset V
finite,
\nu|_{\Lambda}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
,

•

\nu(R)=1
where

R=\Big\{(\varphi_{x})_{x\in V}:\exists C\in(0,\infty),\sum_{x\in B_{k}}\varphi_{x}^{2}\leq C|B_{k}|\text{ for all }k\in\mathbb{N}\Big\}.

(D.2)

Furthermore, recall that a Borel probability measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{V}
is a Gibbs measure at
(\beta,h)
if it is tempered and for every finite
\Lambda\subset V
and
f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
bounded and measurable, the DLR equation

\nu(f)=\int_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{V}}\langle f\rangle^{\eta}_{\Lambda,\beta,h}\textup{d}\nu(\eta)

holds. We call
\mathcal{G}(\beta,h)
the set of Gibbs measures. The main results of this section are the following.

Proposition D.4
.

Let
(\nu_{\Lambda_{n}})_{n\geq 0}
be a sequence of finite volume measures with (random) weakly growing boundary conditions. Then, the sequence is tight and any limit point is a tempered probability measure.

Proposition D.5
.

Let
(\nu_{\Lambda_{n}})_{n\geq 0}
be a sequence of finite volume measure with (random) weakly growing boundary conditions such that
\nu_{\Lambda_{n}}
converges weakly to a tempered probability measure
\nu
. Then,
\nu
is a Gibbs measure, i.e. satisfies the DLR equations.

Proposition D.6
.

For any
(\beta,h)
, there exist
\gamma=\gamma(\beta,h),\delta=\delta(\beta,h)>0
such that every Gibbs measure
\nu\in\mathcal{G}(\beta,h)
is
(\gamma,\delta)
-regular. Moreover, the constants
(\gamma,\delta)
depend continuous on
(\beta,h)
.

The proofs of Propositions
D.4
–
D.6
are based on of the proofs of Theorems 4.3, 4.5, and 4.4 in
[
LP76
]
, respectively
8
8

8

Note that the proof of
[
LP76
, Theorem 4.5]
is given in the appendix of
[
LP76
]
, where it is mislabelled as proof of Theorem 4.4.
, adapted from the case of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to our setting. The extensions of these proofs require us to develop some technical results, which broadly speaking allow us to distinguish as well as quantify bulk and boundary contributions from the potential. The key geometric estimate is contained in Proposition
D.10
. The key probabilistic estimate is contained in Proposition
D.11
, which in particular implies uniform regularity estimates for finite volume measures, see Corollary
D.12
. Both key estimates require multiscale control over the decay and oscillations of the kernel
\Psi
— which in turn controls the decay of the interactions
J
— on scales determined by the geometry of the graph; this naturally imposes conditions jointly on the allowed graphs and interactions. For graphs of polynomial growth, these conditions are explicit. Since, given Proposition
D.11
and Corollary
D.12
, the proofs of Propositions
D.4
–
D.6
are identical to
[
LP76
]
, we omit them and refer to the exact references given above.

D.2
Technical results on bulk-boundary contributions

We first verify that
\Psi
is indeed summable on
G
.

Lemma D.7
.

We have that

\|\Psi\|:=\sup_{x\in V}\sum_{y\in V}\Psi(d_{G}(x,y))=\sum_{y\in V}\Psi(d_{G}(o,y))<\infty.

Proof.

By summation by parts and
\Gamma
-invariance, we have that

\sum_{y\in V}\Psi(d_{G}(x,y))=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\Psi(k)-\Psi(k+1))|B_{k}|.

Note that
\Psi(k)-\Psi(k+1)=O(k^{-(d+1+\varepsilon)})
, from which the result follows.
∎

We are interested in quantifying the oscillatory behaviour of
\Psi
at large scales. In our analysis we require the scales to have a nonlinear dependence depending on the dimension. Let
\alpha=\frac{d}{\varepsilon}+1
and define
F_{n}:=B_{t_{n}}
, where

t_{n}=\lfloor n^{\alpha}\rfloor
. We write
V_{n}=|F_{n}|
. We often refer to
n
as the scale even though, strictly speaking, the scale is
t_{n}
.

For
q,k\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\Psi_{k,q}=\sup_{x\in F_{q},\,y\in V\setminus F_{q+k}}\Psi(d_{G}(x,y)).

The coefficient
\Psi_{k,q}
tracks the oscillation of
\Psi
from scale
q
to scale
q+k
. The next lemma states that the decay condition on
\Psi
is enough to ensure that asymptotically the oscillations are small with respect to the volume.

Lemma D.8
.

We have that

\lim_{q\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\Psi_{k,q}-\Psi_{k+1,q}\right)V_{q+k+1}=0.

(D.3)

Proof.

By summation by parts

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\Psi_{k,q}-\Psi_{k+1,q}\right)V_{q+k+1}\leq\Psi_{1,q}V_{q+2}+C_{G}\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\Psi_{k,q}\left(t_{q+k+1}^{d}-t_{q+k}^{d}\right)

where
C_{G}
is as in (
D.1
).
Note that
\Psi_{k,q}=C_{\Psi}(t_{q+k}+1-t_{q})^{-d-\varepsilon}
. On one hand, by telescoping and a simple calculation

t_{q+k}+1-t_{q}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{q+i}-t_{q+i-1}\geq c_{1}\sum_{i=\lfloor k/2\rfloor}^{k}(q+i)^{\alpha-1}\geq c_{2}k(q+k)^{\alpha-1}

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
, hence
\Psi_{k,q}\leq C_{\Psi}(c_{2}k)^{-d-\varepsilon}(q+k)^{-(\alpha-1)(d+\varepsilon)}
. On the other hand,

t_{q+k+1}^{d}-t_{q+k}^{d}=O((q+k)^{\alpha d-1})
. Since
(\alpha-1)(d+\varepsilon)>\alpha d-1
, the desired assertion follows.
∎

We now introduce a sequence
(\psi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
that plays the role of
C
in (
D.2
) from the definition of tempered measures. It is chosen to increase from scale to scale sufficiently slowly so that the following holds.

Lemma D.9
.

There exists an increasing sequence
(\psi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
converging to infinity such that
\psi_{1}\geq 1
,
\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\psi_{j+1}}{\psi_{j}}=1
,
and

\lim_{q\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\Psi_{k,q}-\Psi_{k+1,q}\right)\psi_{q+k+2}V_{q+k+2}=0.

(D.4)

Proof.

Let
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a strictly increasing sequence of positive integers such that

\sum_{k=m_{n,q}}^{\infty}\left(\Psi_{k,q}-\Psi_{k+1,q}\right)V_{q+k+2}\leq 2^{-n}\quad\text{for every }q\geq 1,

where
m_{n,q}:=\max\{1,k_{n}-q-2\}
. Such a sequence exists because of (
D.3
) together with the fact that
V_{q+k+2}=O(V_{q+k+1})
. Define

\psi_{j}:=\begin{cases}1,&\text{for }j<k_{1}\\
n,&\text{for }k_{n}\leq j<k_{n+1},n\geq 1.\end{cases}

Then,
\psi_{1}\geq 1
,
\psi_{j+1}/\psi_{j}\rightarrow 1
, and for every
q\geq k_{1}-1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\Psi_{k,q}-\Psi_{k+1,q}\right)\psi_{q+k+2}V_{q+k+2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=n(q)}^{\infty}\sum_{k=m_{n,q}}^{m_{n+1,q}-1}\left(\Psi_{k,q}-\Psi_{k+1,q}\right)\psi_{q+k+2}V_{q+k+2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=n(q)}^{\infty}n2^{-n}\rightarrow 0

as
q\rightarrow\infty
, where
n(q):=\max\{n\geq 1\mid m_{n,q}=1\}
.
∎

We now state a technical result which plays an important role in the proof of the key probabilistic estimate in the next section. Loosely speaking, it states that, at the last scale
q
at which the bulk contribution of
\sum_{x\in F_{q}}\varphi_{x}^{2}
dominates the volume, the boundary behaviour on the subsequent scale can be controlled by the bulk behaviour.

Proposition D.10
.

There exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that the following holds. Let
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
. Suppose there exists
q=q(\varphi)\geq 1
such that
q
is the largest integer for which

\sum_{x\in F_{q}}\varphi_{x}^{2}\geq\psi_{q}V_{q}.

Then,

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in F_{q+1}}\sum_{y\in V\setminus F_{q+1}}\Psi(d_{G}(x,y))\left(\varphi_{x}^{2}+\varphi_{y}^{2}\right)\leq C_{1}\sum_{x\in F_{q+1}}\varphi_{x}^{2}.

Proof.

First observe that, for
k\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Psi_{k,q+1}\sum_{x\in F_{q+k+2}\setminus F_{q+k+1}}\varphi_{x}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\Psi_{k,q+1}-\Psi_{k+1,q+1}\right)\sum_{x\in F_{q+k+2}\setminus F_{q+2}}\varphi_{x}^{2}

(D.5)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\Psi_{k,q+1}-\Psi_{k+1,q+1}\right)\psi_{q+k+2}V_{q+k+2}.

We now handle the double sum by taking cases according to the position of
x
and
y
. Let us write

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle:=\sum_{x\in F_{q+1}}\sum_{y\in V\setminus F_{q+1}}\Psi(d_{G}(x,y))\left(\varphi_{x}^{2}+\varphi_{y}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in F_{q+2}\setminus F_{q+1}}\varphi_{y}^{2}\sum_{x\in F_{q+1}}\Psi(d_{G}(x,y))+\sum_{y\in V\setminus F_{q+2}}\varphi_{y}^{2}\sum_{x\in F_{q+1}}\Psi(d_{G}(x,y))

\displaystyle+\sum_{x\in F_{q+1}}\varphi_{x}^{2}\sum_{y\in V\setminus F_{q+1}}\Psi(d_{G}(x,y))

\displaystyle=:D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}.

Observe that, by Lemma
D.7
,

D_{1}+D_{3}\leq\|\Psi\|\sum_{x\in F_{q+2}}\varphi_{x}^{2}\leq\|\Psi\|\psi_{q+2}V_{q+2}.

Since
V_{q+2}=O(V_{q})
and
\psi_{q+2}/\psi_{q}\rightarrow 1
as
q\rightarrow\infty
, it then follows that

D_{1}+D_{3}\leq C_{3}\psi_{q}V_{q}\leq C_{3}\sum_{x\in F_{q+1}}\varphi_{x}^{2}

for some
C_{3}>0
large enough. To handle
D_{2}
,
we bound
\Psi(d_{G}(x,y))
by
\Psi_{k,q+1}
for
x\in F_{q+1}
and
y\in F_{q+k+2}\setminus F_{q+k+1}
to obtain that

D_{2}\leq V_{q+1}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Psi_{k,q}\sum_{y\in F_{q+k+2}\setminus F_{q+k+1}}\varphi_{y}^{2}.

Using (
D.5
) we obtain that

D_{2}\leq V_{q+1}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\Psi_{k,q+1}-\Psi_{k+1,q+1}\right)\psi_{q+k+2}V_{q+k+2}.

By (
D.4
) and the fact that
V_{q+1}=O(V_{q})
we have

D_{2}\leq C_{4}\psi_{q}V_{q}\leq C_{4}\sum_{x\in F_{q+1}}\varphi_{x}^{2}

for some constant
C_{4}>0
.
The desired assertion follows readily.
∎

D.3
Key probabilistic regularity estimates

We now prove the key probabilistic estimates which justify the extension of the proofs of Propositions
D.4
,
D.5
and
D.6
for the case of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to our setting. We state it for deterministic boundary conditions; the proof is exactly the same in the case of random tempered boundary conditions, although with heavier notation.

Proposition D.11
.

Let
q_{0}>0
and let
\Lambda=B_{n}
for
n>t_{q_{0}}
so that
F_{q_{0}}\subset\Lambda
. There exists
c_{1}=c_{1}(q_{0})>0
such that, for all
\Delta\subset\Lambda
with
F_{q_{0}}\subset\Delta\subset\{x\in\Lambda:d_{G}(x,\partial\Lambda)>n^{\delta/2}\}
where
\delta
is as in Remark
D.2
, and for any
x\in\Delta
, the following decomposition holds. For all
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
such that
|\eta_{z}|\leq\mathfrak{p}_{z}
, for all
z\in\Lambda^{c}
, for all
\varphi_{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Delta}
,
\nu_{\Delta}^{(\Lambda),\eta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]=\nu^{1,\eta}_{\Delta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]+\nu^{2,\eta}_{\Delta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]
with

\displaystyle\nu^{1,\eta}_{\Delta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}^{|\Delta|}e^{-\frac{g}{2}\varphi_{x}^{4}}\nu_{\Delta\setminus\{x\}}^{(\Lambda)}[\varphi_{\Delta\setminus\{x\}}],

(D.6)

\displaystyle\nu^{2,\eta}_{\Delta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q\geq q_{0}}e^{-\sum_{z\in F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}\frac{g}{2}\varphi_{z}^{4}+c_{1}V_{q+1}}\,\nu_{\Delta\setminus F_{q+1}(x)}^{(\Lambda)}[\varphi_{\Delta\setminus F_{q+1}(x)}].

(D.7)

We stress that
c_{1}
uniform over all such
\Delta
and
\eta
, and moreover that it is continuous in the parameters of the model.

Proof.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be a sufficiently large constant to be a posteriori. Fix
x\in\Delta
. Write

A_{\Delta,q_{0}}(x)=\{\varphi_{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Delta}:\sum_{y\in F_{q}(x)}\varphi_{y}^{2}\leq\psi_{q}V_{q}\textup{ for all }q\geq q_{0}\},

where
F_{q}(x)=\gamma_{x}F_{q}
where
\gamma_{x}
is an automorphism such that
\gamma_{x}o=x
. Define

\displaystyle\nu^{1,\eta}_{\Delta}[\cdot]

\displaystyle:=\nu_{\Delta}^{(\Lambda),\eta}[\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{A_{\Delta,q_{0}}(x)\}],\qquad\nu^{2,\eta}_{\Delta}:=\nu^{(\Lambda)}_{\Delta}-\nu^{1,\eta}_{\Delta}.

On the event
A_{\Delta,q_{0}}(x)
, we are able to control the bulk behaviour (centred at
x
) of
\varphi_{y}^{2}
in spatial average; on the complement we have control via the oscillation and decay bounds.

We first establish (
D.6
). For
\Lambda=\Lambda_{1}\sqcup\Lambda_{2}
, for all
\varphi^{\prime}_{\Lambda},\varphi_{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
, define

W_{\Lambda}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\Lambda_{1}},\varphi_{\Lambda_{2}}):=U^{\eta}_{\Lambda_{1}}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\Lambda_{1}})+U^{\eta}_{\Lambda_{2}}(\varphi_{\Lambda_{2}})-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{\Lambda_{1}}^{\prime}\sqcup\varphi_{\Lambda_{2}}).

In particular,

W_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{\Lambda_{1}},\varphi_{\Lambda_{2}})=\Big\{\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{1}}+\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{2}}-\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda}\Big\}J_{x,y}\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}=-\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{1},y\in\Lambda_{2}}J_{x,y}\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}.

In particular, observe that for our fixed
x\in\Delta
,

W_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{x},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})=W_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{x}^{\prime},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})-\sum_{y\in\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}J_{x,y}(\varphi_{x}-\varphi_{x}^{\prime})\varphi_{y}.

Thus, by Young’s inequality,

\left|\sum_{y\in\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}J_{x,y}(\varphi_{x}-\varphi_{x}^{\prime})\varphi_{y}\right|\leq\frac{\sum_{y\in\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}J_{x,y}}{2}\Big(\varphi_{x}^{2}+(\varphi^{\prime}_{x})^{2}\Big)+T_{1}

where

T_{1}=\sum_{y\in\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}J_{x,y}\varphi_{y}^{2}\leq\sum_{y\in F_{q_{0}}(x)}J_{x,y}\varphi_{y}^{2}+\sum_{y\in\Lambda\setminus F_{q_{0}}(x)}J_{x,y}\varphi_{y}^{2}:=T_{2}+T_{3}.

By the definition of
A_{\Delta,q_{0}}(x)
,

T_{2}\leq|J|_{\infty}\psi_{q_{0}}V_{q_{0}}.

In order to handle
T_{3}
, we observe that

\sum_{y\in F_{q}(x)}\varphi_{y}^{2}\leq\psi_{q}V_{q}.

for every
q\geq q_{0}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in V\setminus F_{q_{0}}(x)}\Psi(d_{G}(x,y))\varphi_{y}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}\Psi_{k,q_{0}-1}\sum_{y\in F_{q_{0}+k}(x)\setminus F_{q_{0}-1+k}(x)}\varphi_{y}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}(\Psi_{k,q_{0}-1}-\Psi_{k+1,q_{0}-1})\sum_{y\in F_{q_{0}+k}(x)\setminus F_{q_{0}}(x)}\varphi_{y}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}(\Psi_{k,q_{0}-1}-\Psi_{k+1,q_{0}-1})\psi_{q_{0}+k}V_{q_{0}+k},

where in the first inequality we have bounded
\Psi(d_{G}(x,y))
by
\Psi_{k,q_{0}-1}
for
y\in F_{q_{0}+k}(x)\setminus F_{q_{0}-1+k}(x)
(we use that
x\in F_{q_{0}-1}(x)
), then in the second inequality we use a summation by parts. Combining the above estimates together and using Lemma
D.9
, we have established that

T_{3}\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}(\Psi_{k,q_{0}-1}-\Psi_{k+1,q_{0}-1})\psi_{q_{0}+k}V_{q_{0}+k}\leq C_{1}.

All together we have established

|W_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{x},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})-W_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{x}^{\prime},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})|\leq\frac{\|\Psi\|}{2}\big(\varphi_{x}^{2}+(\varphi_{x}^{\prime})^{2}\big)+|J|_{\infty}\psi_{q_{0}}V_{q_{0}}+C_{1}.

We now bound
\nu^{1,\eta}_{\Delta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]
. First observe that, for any
\varphi_{x}^{\prime}\in[-A,A]
, where
A>0
,

\displaystyle\nu^{1,\eta}_{\Delta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]

\displaystyle=Z_{\Lambda}^{-1}\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\{x\}}(\varphi_{x})-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}(\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})+W(\varphi_{x},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}

\displaystyle=e^{-U^{\eta}_{\{x\}}(\varphi_{x})}Z_{\Lambda}^{-1}\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}(\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})+W(\varphi_{x}^{\prime},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})-W(\varphi_{x}^{\prime},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})+W(\varphi_{x},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-U^{\eta}_{\{x\}}(\varphi_{x})}e^{\frac{\sum_{y\in V}J_{0,y}}{2}\Big(\varphi_{x}^{2}+(\varphi^{\prime}_{x})^{2}\Big)}\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}(\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})+W(\varphi_{x}^{\prime},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\frac{g}{2}\varphi_{x}^{4}}\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}\sqcup\varphi^{\prime}_{x})+U^{\eta}_{\{x\}}(\varphi_{x}^{\prime})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\frac{g}{2}\varphi_{x}^{4}}\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}\sqcup\varphi^{\prime}_{x})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}.

Above, the first two lines are straightforward manipulations; the third line follows from the preceding bounds; the fourth line follows from Young’s inequality inside the exponentials, the fact that
|\varphi_{x}^{\prime}|\leq A
, and estimates similar to (
2.8
) to handle the boundary terms in
U^{\eta}_{\{x\}}(\varphi_{x})
; and the final line is by using that
|\varphi_{x}^{\prime}|\leq A
. Thus, taking the integral mean,

\nu^{1,\eta}_{\Delta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]\leq c_{1}^{|\Delta|}e^{-\frac{g}{2}\varphi_{x}^{4}}\int_{-A}^{A}\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}\sqcup\varphi_{x}^{\prime})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}\textup{d}\varphi_{x}^{\prime}\leq c_{1}^{|\Delta|}e^{-\frac{g}{2}\varphi_{x}^{4}}\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{\Lambda})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta\cup\{x\}}.

This last bound establishes (
D.6
).

We now turn to (
D.7
). Write

B_{\Delta,q}(x)=\{\varphi_{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Delta}:\sum_{y\in F_{q}(x)}\varphi_{y}^{2}>\psi_{q}V_{q},\>\sum_{y\in F_{k}(x)}\varphi_{y}^{2}\leq\psi_{k}V_{k},\>\forall k>q\}.

Then, by a union bound and re-ordering terms

\displaystyle\nu^{2,\eta}_{\Delta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q\geq q_{0}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\{\varphi_{\Delta}\in B_{\Delta,q}(x)\}}}{Z_{\Lambda}}\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{\Lambda})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\geq q_{0}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\{\varphi_{\Delta}\in B_{\Delta,q}(x)\}}}{Z_{\Lambda}}\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}(\varphi_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta})-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)}(\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)})}

\displaystyle\times e^{W_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q\geq q_{0}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\{\varphi_{\Delta}\in B_{\Delta,q}\}}}{Z_{\Lambda}}e^{-\sum_{y\in F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}\frac{3g}{4}\varphi_{y}^{4}+CV_{q+1}}

\displaystyle\times\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)}(\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)})+W_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}

where the third line is uses similar reasoning as above to handle
U^{\eta}_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}(\varphi_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta})
.

Let
\varphi_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}
be such that
|\varphi^{\prime}_{z}|\leq A
for all
z\in F_{q+1}(z)\cap\Delta
. Observe that we can write

W_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)})=W(\varphi_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}^{\prime},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)})+T_{4},

where

|T_{4}|\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta\\
y\in\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)\end{subarray}}\Psi(d_{G}(z,y))\Big(\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{z}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\varphi_{z}^{\prime})^{2}+\varphi_{y}^{2}\Big)

(D.8)

On the event
B_{\Delta,q}(x)
, we can apply Proposition
D.10
and
\Gamma
-invariance to obtain

\eqref{eq: t4}\leq C_{2}\sum_{z\in F_{q+1}\cap\Delta}\varphi_{z}^{2}+C_{3}\sum_{z\in F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}(\varphi_{z}^{\prime})^{2}.

Thus, by Young’s inequality, using the boundedness of
\varphi_{x}^{\prime}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\nu^{2,\eta}_{\Delta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q\geq q_{0}}\frac{1}{Z_{\Lambda}}e^{-\sum_{y\in F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}\frac{g}{2}\varphi_{y}^{4}+C_{4}V_{q+1}}

\displaystyle\times\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)}(\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)})+W_{\Lambda}(\varphi^{\prime}_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta},\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\geq q_{0}}\frac{1}{Z_{\Lambda}}e^{-\sum_{y\in F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}\frac{g}{2}\varphi_{y}^{4}+C_{4}V_{q+1}}

\displaystyle\times\int e^{-U^{\eta}_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus(F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta)}\sqcup\varphi^{\prime}_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta})+U^{\eta}_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}(\varphi^{\prime}_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta})}\textup{d}\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\Delta}.

The desired estimate follows by using the boundedness of
|\varphi^{\prime}_{F_{q+1}(x)\cap\Delta}|
and taking the integral mean with respect to
\varphi_{F_{q+1}\cap\Lambda}^{\prime}
, similarly as for the estimate on
\nu^{1,\eta}_{\Delta}
.
∎

Corollary D.12
.

There exists
c_{1}>0
such that the following holds. Let
n\geq 1
and let
\Lambda\subset V
be finite such that
\Lambda\supset B_{n}
. Let
\Delta\subset\Lambda
such that
\Delta\subset B_{m}
, where
m={n-n^{\frac{\delta}{2}}}
and
\delta
is as in Remark
D.2
. Then, for all
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
such that
|\eta_{z}|\leq\sqrt{\log d_{G}(o,\partial\Lambda)}
for all
z\in\Lambda^{c}
,

\nu_{\Delta}^{(\Lambda),\eta}[\varphi_{\Delta}]\leq e^{-\sum_{x\in\Delta}\big(\frac{g}{2}\varphi_{x}^{4}-c_{1}\big)}.

Proof.

The result is based on an induction argument that, given Proposition
D.11
, is identical to the proof of
[
Rue70
, Theorem 2.2]
. We omit the details.
∎

Remark D.13
.

The result of Corollary
D.12
can be extended up to the boundary with the cost of setting
c_{1}=O(\log d_{G}(o,\partial\Lambda))
. In particular, by using an exponential Chebyschev argument, one can bound moments; for instance for some
C>0
we have

\sup_{\Delta\subset\Lambda}\sup_{x\in\Delta}\nu^{(\Lambda),\eta}_{\Delta}[\varphi_{x}^{2}]\leq\sqrt{C\log d_{G}(o,\partial\Lambda)}.

D.4
Generalisation to tempered amenable graphs

The results of this section can be generalised to (supposedly) a larger class of amenable graphs that we now introduce. Recall that a connected, locally finite, and vertex-transitive graph
G=(V,E)
is called amenable if

\inf_{A\subset V,|A|<\infty}\frac{|\partial A|}{|A|}=0.

(D.9)

Equivalently,
G
is amenable if and only if it admits a Følner sequence
(F_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset V
, i.e. a sequence
(F_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\frac{|\partial F_{n}|}{|F_{n}|}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. We call
(F_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
a Følner exhaustion if it is a Følner sequence and, in addition,
F_{n}\uparrow V
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

We say that a connected, locally finite, vertex-transitive graph
G
is a
tempered amenable graph
if it admits a function
f:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
such that
f(x+y)\leq f(x)f(y)
and a Følner exhaustion
(F_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{x\in V\setminus\{o\}}\frac{1}{f(d_{G}(o,x))}<\infty\end{split}

(D.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{|F_{n+1}|}{|F_{n}|}<\infty\end{split}

(D.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}F_{n}\cup\partial F_{n}\subset F_{n+1}\end{split}

(D.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{(|F_{n+1}|-|F_{n}|)\max_{x\in F_{n+1}\setminus F_{n}}\{\sqrt[4]{\log f(d_{G}(o,x))}\}}{|F_{n+1}|}=0.\end{split}

(D.13)

We restrict to kernels
\Psi:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
which are decreasing and satisfy

\lim_{q\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\Psi_{k,q}-\Psi_{k+1,q}\right)|F_{q+k+1}|=0.

(D.14)

Moreover, we define

\Xi(M)=\{\varphi:\exists V_{\varphi}\subset V,|\varphi_{x}|\leq\sqrt[4]{M\log f(d_{G}(o,x))}\,\forall x\in V_{\varphi}^{c}\}.

(D.15)

Then, one can check that the proofs of Propositions
D.4
–
D.6
extend to this setting.

Remark D.14
.

Any connected, vertex-transitive graph of polynomial growth is a tempered amenable graph. It would be interesting to find amenable graphs of intermediate or exponential growth that satisfy our assumptions; we do not know of any such examples as of yet.
